Personal Branding From the Buyer's Side of the Desk: What Clients Actually Check Before Hiring

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 16 • Originally published at tanit365.com

Most advice about personal branding is written from the freelancer's side of the desk: pick a niche, post consistently, grow an audience. This piece flips the camera around and looks at the same problem from the buyer's side — what a potential client actually scans for in the ten minutes between opening your profile and deciding whether to reply, hire, or move on. The original guide on tanit365.com lays out the full twelve-week system; here we focus on the handful of details that decide most real hiring decisions.

A client evaluating two freelancers is not comparing follower counts. They are running one silent question: has this person solved a problem like mine, and can they show it? A profile with twelve thoughtful posts and no documented work loses to a thin profile with two detailed case studies.

So before touching any platform, build an evidence inventory: every project you have finished, written down as situation, problem, action, measurable outcome, and duration. Even two modest projects documented this way give a buyer more to evaluate than a month of posting. If you have no client work yet, document one self-initiated or discounted project in public — one real teardown with numbers beats weeks of opinion posts, because evidence is the only content a buyer can verify.

Strip away the branding language and a hiring decision is a short checklist, usually run unconsciously:

Notice that four of the five are answered by artifacts, not activity. Publishing helps, but it is the last mile, not the road.

The three-page home base from the original guide works because it mirrors how buyers scan. Order the sections the way a client reads: positioning statement first, proof second, terms third, contact last. Two details most freelancers omit and buyers quietly look for:

Technical notes that matter more than they seem to: a custom domain rather than a platform subdomain, a page that loads fast on a phone because most first visits are mobile, and a visible email address, since some corporate buyers cannot submit external forms from their networks. A plain static page on affordable hosting does the whole job; spending a week choosing a framework is a week of positioning and proof not being built.

The cadence advice in the original guide — one substantial piece every two weeks plus short updates, sustainable inside three to five hours a week — survives contact with reality for one reason: reuse. Freelancers who publish consistently are usually not writing more than anyone else; they are repurposing better. One case study becomes a short update, a section of the work page, an answer in a client conversation, and a paragraph in a proposal.

A twelve-week cycle makes this measurable. Review at weeks four, eight, and twelve — but from the buyer's side the only review question that matters is: which pieces produced enquiries? Shift toward those. If eight weeks of consistent publishing produces no enquiries at all, the problem is almost never effort; it is that the positioning answers a question nobody is asking. Narrow the audience until the sentence makes a specific person think "that is me."

The brand moment most freelancers neglect is the first reply. Answer enquiries within one business day, and make the reply itself evidence: one line acknowledging the project, two questions about scope and deadline, one proposed call slot. Asking about the outcome, deadline, and budget range before sending a price turns the conversation from "is it too expensive" into "which option fits" — and lets you reduce scope instead of price when budget pushes back. Cutting price on the same scope teaches the client your first number was decorative.

At the moment of delivery, while goodwill peaks, ask for two things: a short testimonial and one introduction to someone with a similar problem. Those two requests replace most cold outreach a freelancer would otherwise do next quarter.

Working signals: qualified enquiries that mention your positioning language back to you, enquiries arriving from the proof pages rather than the profile, and clients referencing a specific case study. Noise: impressions, follower growth, and engagement on posts that never mentions your service. A brand that produces applause but no enquiries is entertainment, not marketing; tighten the positioning sentence and publish the price anchor before posting anything new.

Social platforms rent you attention and can change the rent overnight. The durable part of a personal brand is the home base — the domain, the case studies, the record of finished work. Keep it small, keep it fast, keep it yours, and update it on the twelve-week rhythm rather than rebuilding it every quarter. That is what makes the right client find you, verify you, and pay you without a bidding war.

The full twelve-week system — positioning statement, channel choice, page structure, cadence, conversion path, and review checkpoints — is on tanit365.com. This article is an independent companion piece written for the dev.to community; no syndicated text, all angles re-examined from the buyer's side.

How long before a personal brand actually produces clients? Plan on months, not weeks: the first stretch builds the habit and the home base, the middle builds search and referral surface, and enquiries usually start once the proof inventory is deep enough for a buyer to evaluate you in one sitting.

One platform or many? Two channels: one discovery, one credibility, typically your own site. Five half-active profiles read as an incomplete story; two consistent ones read as a professional.

What if I have no client results to show? Publish process and trade-off content, and get one documented project — even free or discounted — in exchange for the right to write it up. That single project generates more enquiries than a quarter of opinion posts.

Auditable AI billing without a blockchain: signed batches and their limits

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 16 • Originally published at teerouter.hashnode.dev

The difficult part of AI API billing is often the request that almost finished. The router sent the prompt, the provider began responding, and the connection disappeared before final usage arrived. A database can store a precise number for that request without making the number well justified.

I am building TeeRouter, and this led to two separate design questions: what evidence supports a charge, and how can someone later detect changes to the published accounting records?

The implementation borrows a useful idea associated with blockchains: publish cryptographically verifiable records that readers can inspect independently. It does not run a blockchain or claim decentralized consensus. This article examines the public source at commit 5c89b551; it is an implementation case study, not an independent certification of the deployed service.

A receipt should preserve the pricing decision used for that request. Looking up today's price when displaying last week's usage makes historical charges difficult to reproduce.

TeeRouter freezes the pricing revision, applicable rules, and pool multiplier at admission. Provider-specific calculators interpret native usage fields. The basis is public API list pricing multiplied by the pool's multiplier, not a claim about the merchant's actual subscription expenditure or negotiated invoice.

The billing implementation uses integer arithmetic, retains pre-rounding amounts, and rounds upward once per request to micro-USD. This makes the chosen calculation reproducible without promising to match a provider's monthly invoice rounding, taxes, or discounts.

For a simple illustrative quote, 10,000 ordinary input tokens at $2 per million plus 1,000 output tokens at $8 per million produces a $0.028 base cost. A 1.2 multiplier gives $0.0336. These are made-up rates, not a current model quotation. Real records must also distinguish cached input, applicable pricing dimensions, and how each provider reports usage.

The interrupted-request policy separates several outcomes:

A generated refusal inside a successful model response is different from an HTTP rejection: generation may have consumed billable tokens.

The controversial row is the timeout after a full flush. A successful local flush does not prove that the remote application received or executed the request. The policy chooses a financial treatment for that ambiguity; it does not eliminate the ambiguity. Customers need to know that an estimated input charge can occur at this boundary.

Estimation is restricted to failed or interrupted model requests and is labelled estimated or mixed, with partial coverage. Unknown hidden output is not invented. A downstream cancellation cancels the upstream operation rather than draining it in the background, although closing a connection cannot guarantee the provider immediately stops computing.

Publishing these distinctions matters more than making every row appear confidently “settled.” A signed estimate remains an estimate.

The current public receipt format signs batches of records. A manifest includes the boot and key identity, batch number, previous hash, sequence range, record digest, count, and calculated total.

Conceptually, verification follows this chain:

There is one signature per batch. The current public format does not have per-record Merkle inclusion proofs. A reader needs the original complete records_json to verify the records; reserializing JSON may change the bytes being checked.

The verifier distinguishes a valid signature from an independently trusted TEE signing identity. The latter also requires hardware evidence and an approved reference policy. It is not established by a public key or a “verified” label included in the downloaded bundle.

Hash links let readers check continuity between supplied batches. They do not create consensus, prove that an operator has disclosed the latest batch, or prove that no other history exists. Independently retaining known batch heads could strengthen monitoring, but that is a separate system property, not something the signature supplies automatically.

Consider a batch that reaches external storage, followed by a lost acknowledgment. Regenerating it with new metadata would complicate duplicate detection. TeeRouter freezes the signed bytes before upload and resends those same bytes on a later cycle.

A valid durable-storage acknowledgment permits releasing the uploaded raw details. It does not reset consumption or update user balances. Per-user, per-batch summaries remain until a separate settlement acknowledgment applies a balance update through an identified uploaded head.

That boundary is necessary because requests may finish while settlement is happening. Retiring consumption only through the acknowledged head preserves newer charges. Revisions and idempotent retries prevent repeating the same settlement from applying it twice.

The default upload cycle is five minutes. That is a delivery schedule, not a guarantee of completeness or immediate publication.

The optional accounting journal stores committed events and delivery state in SQLite WAL with authenticated encryption. Frozen batches keep their original signatures after restart; new batches use the new process identity.

It protects successfully committed records. It cannot reconstruct an in-flight request that never produced an event, recover usage that was never observed, or prevent deletion or rollback of an entire journal copy. On a journal write failure or capacity limit, the implementation closes new admission instead of silently discarding accepted events.

This makes durability a concrete, bounded claim rather than “nothing can ever be lost.”

Public batches exclude prompts, answers, credentials, and refusal text. They contain accounting fields and refusal categories. User pseudonyms rotate using a fresh public batch nonce and a private random identity, rather than hashing a short username.

That reduces direct linkage across batches but does not guarantee anonymity. Timing, model choices, merchant identifiers, and distinctive traffic volumes can still correlate activity.

The resulting ledger is useful because readers can check the bytes they received, inspect charge evidence, and distinguish estimates from observed usage. Deposits, withdrawals, externally managed balances, missing history, and provider-side execution remain separate questions. Keeping those boundaries explicit is what makes the evidence meaningful.

Writing disclosure: This article was drafted by an AI assistant from the linked public source code and protocol documentation. The account owner maintains TeeRouter.

SMTP 554 5.7.1: blocklist rejection – how to fix

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 16 • Originally published at daniele-michelin.com

The receiver permanently rejects the connection or message because the sending IP is on a DNS blocklist (DNSBL) or the content was classified as spam.

Check the IP at Spamhaus (octets reversed)

Find accounts with suspiciously many logins/mails

What does a 127.255.255.254 answer from Spamhaus mean?

It is not a listing but an error: the query came through a public or open resolver that Spamhaus does not answer.

For Spamhaus SBL/XBL usually minutes to hours after the request, as long as the spam has stopped. Some lists also expire automatically after a few days.

What a TEE can actually prove about an AI API router

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 16 • Originally published at teerouter.hashnode.dev

An AI API router sits in an awkward place. It needs to read a request to select an upstream account, enforce permissions, and calculate usage. That also gives its operator opportunities to change parameters, misuse credentials, or report a different price from the one the client expected.

Publishing the router's source helps people inspect those decisions. It leaves another question unanswered: is that the program handling my request?

I am building TeeRouter, an open-source router that explores this question using trusted execution environments and independently verifiable receipts. This is a case study of its public implementation at commit 5c89b551, rather than an independent audit or verification of a live deployment. The interesting part is how narrow the useful guarantees need to be.

Suppose a service presents a valid hardware attestation report. If the report is unrelated to the TLS connection carrying your prompts, it tells you very little about where those prompts go. An untrusted frontend could show evidence from a legitimate enclave while processing requests somewhere else.

TeeRouter binds its current TLS certificate and a client-generated challenge into the attestation report. The binding in the channel implementation is:

The nonce gives the verifier a freshness challenge. The certificate digest connects the evidence to the certificate used for TLS. The protocol label separates this purpose from other uses of attestation data.

This only works if the client actually checks that binding and uses the corresponding authenticated connection. Downloading a report, displaying a green badge, and then sending traffic to an unrelated endpoint would discard the useful property.

An authentic report can still describe a program you should not trust. A verifier needs an independently chosen policy defining which measurements and security state it accepts.

For the TDX path, the project checks certificate chains, revocation and trusted computing base information, measurements, the relevant event sequence, and the report-data binding. The security document makes the independence requirement explicit: evidence supplied by the server cannot also be the sole authority deciding what counts as trustworthy.

There is an operational consequence here. Updating the router is also a trust-policy problem. A client must have a defensible reason to accept the new measured software. Automatically trusting every measurement announced by the service would weaken the point of checking measurements at all.

The application also matters. A correctly attested program with a credential-export endpoint would still export credentials. TEE isolation does not repair bad authorization logic, unsafe dependencies, or mistakes in the approved code. It reduces particular infrastructure trust assumptions under its hardware and software threat model; it does not remove the need for review.

A router administrator needs to change balances and permissions. That does not mean the administrator should be able to impersonate a user and spend their balance.

The current user authentication protocol requires a client-owned Ed25519 signature as well as a valid Bearer credential. A user ID is derived from the public key; the signing seed stays with the client.

The signature covers the exact body bytes, method, path and query, relevant headers, a timestamp, a random nonce, and the router's boot ID. Including authorization and routing headers prevents someone from preserving the body while silently changing the account-pool selection or other signed instructions. Including a boot ID makes proofs from an earlier process invalid after a restart.

This is a deliberately limited separation of powers. An administrator can still deny service, change permission settings, or alter externally managed balances. What administrator authority alone cannot do, under the verified implementation, is produce a valid signature for the existing user's key.

AI requests carry more financially significant settings than a model name. Cache controls, service tiers, reasoning options, and tool definitions can all affect behavior or cost.

TeeRouter's documented forwarding boundary preserves native generation parameters instead of silently adding its own cache or retention choices. Account egress is explicitly configured, official upstream HTTPS certificates remain checked, and a failed proxy is not supposed to fall back to a direct connection. These are application properties to inspect in the approved version, not properties hardware provides automatically.

There is also a boundary the router cannot cross: the upstream provider still receives the prompt. Attesting an intermediary does not attest the provider's internal inference service, prove which model weights it ran, or establish how it retains requests. A router can preserve a requested parameter; it cannot turn that parameter into independently observed behavior inside another company's infrastructure.

The project also signs consumption batches. Its standalone verifier distinguishes signature verification, record verification, and TEE verification.

That distinction is useful beyond this project. A mathematically valid signature says that a key signed particular bytes. Hardware evidence and an approved reference policy can establish more about that key's origin. Neither result alone establishes that every request appears in the supplied history or that the latest batch has been disclosed.

For someone assessing a deployment, I would want separate answers to these questions:

Those answers are more useful than a single “TEE verified” label. The practical value is being able to inspect a specific program, verify a connection to an accepted instance, and understand exactly which remaining promises still require trust.

Disk full but no big files to be found: tracking down deleted, still-open files with lsof

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 16 • Originally published at daniele-michelin.com

"The disk is full, I already deleted the big log file – but not a single byte was freed." A classic. The file is gone from the directory, but its content still takes up space.

On Linux, rm only removes the directory entry, the name. The file system releases the data only when no name points to the file and no process has it open any more.

If a service keeps a file open – typically a web server, a database or an application with its log file – the space stays allocated. Often the process even keeps writing to it. The file just can't be reached through a path any more.

That explains the disagreement between two tools: df asks the file system how much space is used and includes the file. du walks the directories and no longer finds it.

If the numbers are far apart, lsof shows the suspects. The +L1 option lists open files with a link count below one, i.e. already deleted:

The interesting columns are COMMAND, PID, FD, SIZE/OFF and the path marked (deleted). To limit the output to one file system, append the mount point:

Reload or restart the service. Usually the cleanest way. Many services reopen their log files on reload, for example:

A reload only helps if the service reopens its logs while doing it. Otherwise a restart is needed – on production systems, with care and at a suitable time.

Truncate the file through /proc. If a restart isn't possible right now, the deleted file can be truncated to zero bytes through its file descriptor. PID and FD number come from the lsof output (for FD 4w, the number is 4):

This is a stopgap: the content is gone afterwards, so be sure it really is a log file you can do without.

Not every gap between df and du has this cause. Other suspects: files hidden under a mount point mounted on top of them later, and on ext4 the blocks reserved for root. And if df -h shows free space but nothing can be written, check df -i – the inodes may be exhausted.

A full disk is often just the symptom. The cause is frequently a log nobody rotates – a good example of why the second look pays off.

Originally published at daniele-michelin.com. More guides, free checks (SPF, TLS, headers) and an error-message lexicon: daniele-michelin.com/en/.

I watched Scotland sneak past Wallabies and noted two big replacement plus Rangers stars in stands

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Scotland got back on the winning trail with a 15-12 victory over Australia at Murrayfield.

After the disappointment of losing heavily to New Zealand eight days ago, head coach Gregor Townsend will have been pleased to see his team recover - although the triumph was far from swashbuckling.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Mark Atkinson was at Murrayfield to cover proceedings for The Scotsman and picks out some talking points from the match:

Winning was clearly the most important thing for Scotland, which is why with eight minutes to go, Finn Russell took the three points from a penalty rather than going for touch to push for a try.

It moved Scotland ten points clear and left Australia with too much to do, even with Max Jorgensen’s late try to keep the game very close.

However, Scotland’s inability to rack up the points is likely to harm them in the wider scale of the Nations Championship.

With one round of fixtures remaining before finals weekend, Scotland sit fourth in the standings on 15 points with a points difference of -8. After their historic win over New Zealand, fifth-placed Wales also have 15 points but with a points difference of -28. They face Australia next, which is winnable.

Scotland take on whipping boys Japan at Murrayfield on Saturday and need to win comprehensively with a bonus point to put pressure on third-placed Ireland and second-placed England, who are on 20 points and have points differences of +57 and +134 respectively.

Ireland take on South Africa and England face New Zealand, where a solitary bonus point will ensure either of them a top-three finish - and given England’s point difference, it would take something special to overhaul them. Scotland's best hope is probably a big Springbok win.

The chances are Scotland will finish fourth - which would mean a play-off against either Argentina or Australia.

Scotland did not play with much verve or pizzazz and were lamentably wasteful at the start of the second half when Billy Pollard was sent to the sin bin. Rather than adding to the scoreboard, they gave away two cheap penalties and lost their own lineout in a promising position.

Playmaker Russell failed to ignite the game and was patchy with some of his kicking, while the ball very rarely made it out to the wide players.

It wasn’t a game that got the pulses racing and Scotland will likely pay the price.

While both teams will rue not scoring a bucketload of points, there will be some satisfaction from the defensive effort given what happened the previous weekend.

Scotland shipped 43 points to the All Blacks and Australia were on the end of 45 points at Twickenham. It was evident that both sides were intent on keeping the door shut.

While imagination was at a premium, there were a lot of good tackles and discipline when pressure was on. Australia boss Les Kiss referenced it afterwards and Townsend will be pleased with the way his team held firm in difficult conditions for the majority of the final 20 minutes.

Darcy Graham’s tackle right at the end on Tom Wright ensured the win for Scotland and must be applauded for his work at the death.

Scotland had to turn to their bench before and after kick-off, and the replacements did themselves no harm.

Tom Jordan was a last-minute sub for captain Sione Tuipulotu, who failed a late fitness test due to a knee injury. The Bristol Bears man is a versatile fellow and while he’s more often than not been used at 10 or 15 for Scotland, he was asked to partner Huw Jones at centre.

This wasn’t a game for creativity, but Jordan did have some nice offloads and he defended well. If Tuipulotu doesn’t make next week against Japan, Jordan has probably kept himself ahead of Stafford McDowall.

In the pack, Scott Cummings pulled up on 25 minutes and the lock was replaced by his Glasgow Warriors club-mate Max Williamson, who made his presence felt with some big hits and powerful work at the breakdown.

Williamson has found himself behind Cummings and another Glasgow man in Gregor Brown, but this was a performance that will give Townsend food for thought for the rest of the year.

Blair Kinghorn used to be a nailed-on starter for Scotland, but his own form at Toulouse, niggling injuries and the emergence of Kyle Rowe at Glasgow Warriors has put his position in jeopardy.

Townsend gave Kinghorn the whole match at full-back and he served up a reminder of why he can make a difference in the Test arena.

He was secure under the high ball, ran with purpose and broke the line on several occasions. He also put in an excellent tackle on Jorgensen at a crucial point in the game.

Many feel Rowe was unfortunate not to feature against Australia, but Kinghorn has that little bit extra physicality and oomph that sets him apart.

We are also in the midst of an international break in football and that allowed a couple of Rangers players to take in this match.

Scotland striker Lawrence Shankland and Australia midfielder Cammy Devlin are both currently injured, so they were able to watch on in the Murrayfield stands without any commitments - and not far from their old stomping ground.

The ex-Hearts duo seemed to be enjoying the game and Shankland will no doubt have liked Scotland landing the bragging rights over the Wallabies.

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Another chatbot debate experiment with expected (and unexpected) results

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I get way too much enjoyment out of seeing how chatbots respond to my requests about ranking certain things.

I was intrigued by the results given to me after observing ChatGPT, Gemini, Claude and Perplexity’s rankings for the top 10 jobs most likely to be replaced by AI (my interest was equally piqued after seeing their rankings for the jobs currently deemed as “AI-proof”). And when this year’s World Chocolate Day came around, I just had to see which chocolate bar would take over the top spot when I asked those same four chatbots to rank the best ones from around the world.

Since another holiday centered around food is now upon us (National Fast Food Day), I figured it was time for me to present another ranked list challenge to my favorite AI debaters: “Rank the 10 best fast-food restaurants in the US and explain your reasons for each.”

To my surprise, only two of them listed the same spot as their top choice. While the two other chatbots went in a completely different direction with their #1 picks.

ChatGPT kicked things off by highlighting some less-than-surprising picks and a few odd choices that stuck out like a sore thumb to me. Here are the fast-food chains it ranked from 10 to two:

10. Panda Express — Best alternative to burgers and sandwiches

9. Raising Cane’s — Best chicken-tender specialist

Sign up to the Tom's AI Guide weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editors and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

7. Shake Shack — Best premium burger option

6. Popeyes — Best for bold chicken flavor

5. Taco Bell — Best for variety and customization

3. Chick-fil-A — Best dependable chicken stop

2. In-N-Out Burger — Best focused burger menu

I wasn’t expecting Panda Express to make an appearance on a list of the best fast food restaurants. And as someone who was severely disappointed by Raising Cane’s the first time I had it, I was taken aback to see it get a mention on ChatGPT’s list. As for the rest of the chatbot’s choices, they all made sense to me. At the top of ChatGPT’s ranked list was Culver’s, which it marked as the best overall. Its reasoning for selecting that fast-food chain as the absolute best centered around it meeting all of the chatbot’s specific standards (value, consistency, menu variety and service):

ButterBurgers, cheese curds, and frozen custard make a compelling combination, and the broader menu gives families plenty of choices. It earns first place because the whole meal feels considered, from the main course through dessert. ACSI’s 2026 study also highlights its made-to-order food and friendly service.

Gemini’s list was determined by four main benchmarks: food quality, consistency, value and overall customer satisfaction. And just like ChatGPT, its top 10 was impacted by the same benchmarks measured by the American Customer Satisfaction Index. Gemini’s choices also took general customer sentiment of each fast-food spot into account.

This time around, a different restaurant received the honor of being chosen as #1, while ChatGPT’s top selection got knocked down to #3. Here’s how Gemini’s rankings looked from 10 to two:

Gemini gave valid reasons for each of its picks, such as In-N-Out Burger’s peak freshness when it comes to its made-to-order food, fair pricing practices and respectable menu choices. It even mentioned the “Double-Double Animal Style” cheeseburger as the standout item on its menu. As for which fast-food chain emerged as #1, it turned out to be one that I frequently go to during my lunch breaks:

Chick-fil-A. The Reason: Consistency and hospitality set the standard here. Even with massive drive-thru volumes, order accuracy and speed are best-in-class. The Standout: The classic pressure-cooked, peanut-oil-fried chicken sandwich, waffle fries, and house Chick-fil-A sauce.

Claude stepped up to the (deep-fried) plate to offer its rankings, which were determined by each restaurant’s food quality, consistency, value, service, and signature approach to making food. It also considered customer satisfaction surveys and the general reputation of each fast-food chain when listing its picks.

After taking a quick peek at its honorable mentions (which included Shake Shack, Panda Express, Jersey Mike’s and Dunkin’), I came away slightly shocked by two of its choices (since they weren’t mentioned by ChatGPT and Gemini) and less surprised by the other picks that made Claude’s list:

When I glanced at which fast-food chain earned the top spot on Claude’s list, it ended up being the very same place that Gemini chose: Chick-fil-A. Claude’s explanation as to why it also chose that spot is as follows: It consistently tops customer satisfaction surveys. The chicken sandwich is reliably good, service is fast and friendly even with long drive-thru lines, and restaurants are unusually clean and well run.

And for the final ranked list of the finest fast-food establishments, Perplexity considered its choice based on the following criteria: food quality & flavor, value, consistency, menu distinctiveness and convenience. Here are the restaurants that got a mention on Perplexity’s list, from 10 to two (it was funny to see Perplexity agree with Gemini’s choice for the tenth spot):

To my surprise, Chick-fil-A didn’t earn another top spot—Perplexity was far more enamored with In-N-Out Burger as the best fast-food chain in America. And as for why it chose that option, it highlighted the following reason:

My best overall choice for a focused burger experience. Its appeal is a tightly defined menu built around burgers, with customizations such as Animal Style adding variety without turning it into an everything-for-everyone chain. Time Out also ranks it No. 1. Start with a Double-Double, Animal Style.

Gemini and Claude agree that Chick-fil-A is second to none in the competitive race for who makes the best fast food in the nation. ChatGPT chose Culver’s as its fast-food champion, while Perplexity went in a completely different direction by choosing In-N-Out Burger as the top-of-the-line fast-food restaurant. Seeing my personal faves, such as Zaxby’s, Bojangles and Cook Out, get excluded from their lists was a total bummer, but I can see how they may pale in comparison to their more recognizable competitors.

Even though there’s no Culver’s or In-N-Out Burger located in my home state of New York, I’ll be sure to celebrate National Fast Food Day by stuffing my face with some Chick-fil-A (that new chicken & waffles sandwich is everything it’s been hyped up to be and more).

The S&P 500 Looks Cheaper Than It Has in Over 3 Years, but There’s a Catch

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Earnings expectations are rising faster than stock prices this year.

Despite some bumps in the road, the last three years have been fantastic for stock investors. The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.40%) index is up about 80% since the start of November 2023, fueled by the big tech stocks at the forefront of the artificial intelligence (AI) industry. Despite the surge in stock prices, the index looks just as cheap by one measure as it was over three years ago.

The S&P 500 saw its forward P/E ratio fall to 17.7 in the postmidterm rout on Nov. 5. That now sits well below the five- and 10-year average forward P/E ratios for the index (19.8 and 19.1, respectively), and it's the lowest level the metric has reached since Nov. 1, 2023, according to data and analytics company FactSet.

Stocks have recovered a bit from the recent sell-off, and their valuations remain relatively attractive compared to where they've traded in recent years. Before investors expect another 80% climb in stock prices over the next three years, though, there's a big catch they should be aware of.

The forward P/E is a measure made with imperfect information. While we can know the price of stocks (P) with 100% certainty, the earnings (E) half of the equation is based on analysts' best guesses. When those guesses change as we gain new information, the P/E ratio can change significantly. For example, the S&P 500's forward P/E ratio fell from a relatively high 22 at the start of the year to less than 18 today, even as the index climbed.

2026 earnings came in well above expectations at the start of the year, prompting analysts to raise their outlooks for future earnings growth. Actual earnings between the third quarter of last year and the second quarter of this year exceeded analyst expectations by an average of 13.8%. For the third quarter, companies have reported earnings about 2.7% above estimates, which is less than the usual earnings surprise. But analysts already have high expectations of about 30% earnings growth.

Those high expectations carry into the fourth quarter, when analysts expect 28.4% earnings growth, and slow down slightly in the first quarter of next year to 19.5%. Second-quarter forecasts fell to just 3% earnings growth, as some big companies face tough comparables due to one-time investment gains in 2026, but analysts expect 16.1% full-year earnings growth for the S&P 500 in 2027.

Analysts have raised their growth expectations considerably from the start of the year, pushing the forward P/E ratio lower. The big catch, of course, is that future earnings expectations are far from knowable. Even after the stellar earnings results of the last few quarters, the S&P 500's trailing P/E (using actual financial results) of around 25 remains above the five- and 10-year averages (24.4 and 23.7, respectively).

While it's impossible to know if earnings growth can match the torrid pace analysts currently expect, stock investors earn higher returns over the long run because they're willing to accept that risk.

Some argue that analysts' earnings expectations still underappreciate the massive growth opportunity for artificial intelligence. With big tech companies piling growing amounts of cash into their data center build-outs, further revenue growth acceleration could be on the horizon as income-producing assets come online.

On the other side of the coin, some see significant hurdles for further growth among the AI stocks, with capital markets getting tighter and increased pressure from regulators. That could slow down the build-out and bring revenue growth to a halt.

While big AI stocks have the biggest impact on the S&P 500's overall earnings expectations, there are hundreds of companies that are mostly unaffected by the artificial intelligence build-out. Investors willing to dig into individual stocks and their earnings outlooks could find underappreciated opportunities as everyone focuses on AI.

Most importantly, with stock prices relatively low compared to earnings expectations, there's room for error. Stocks still look attractive after an 80% climb since they were last valued this low, but it's important to note that earnings growth expectations are higher now than they were three years ago. That leaves more room to disappoint.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 823%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 209% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

Italia, Donnarumma guarda avanti: "Mancini ci darà tanto per le qualificazioni all'Europeo"

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Al termine del match perso 4-1 contro il Belgio, il capitano dell'Italia Gianluigi Donnarumma ha commentato ai microfoni di SportMediaset:

"Ci tenevamo tanto ad andare avanti e giocarci le finali. Però purtroppo loro sono stati più bravi di noi. Bisogna riconoscerlo. Dovevamo fare meglio, sicuramente mentalmente e fisicamente eravamo un po' stanchi dalla partita contro la Francia dove abbiamo dato tutto. Loro sono stati più bravi, hanno avuto molte palle gol. Alla fine potevamo arrivare primi o secondi ma sfortunatamente siamo arrivati terzi. Fa parte del calcio e di un percorso di crescita intrapreso qualche settimana fa. Rialzeremo la testa e il mister saprà darci tanto per le qualificazioni all'Europeo".

Per trovare la mentalità di quella Nazionale che ha vinto l'Europeo quanto ci vuole? "Secondo me bisogna che i giovani crescano e ci diano una mano. Il calcio europeo ha un ritmo alto, c'è bisogno di esperienza e secondo me bisogna essere più cattivi. Va bene un calcio offensivo ma ci deve essere la voglia di non prendere gol. E con il Belgio ci è mancata. Lo analizzeremo con il mister al prossimo raduno".

Four volunteers spent 378 days inside a windowless NASA Mars habitat

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Four NASA CHAPEA volunteers left a 3D-printed Mars habitat in Houston on Oct. 31, 2026, after 378 days.

Ross Elder, Ellen Ellis, James Spicer and Matthew Montgomery walked out of NASA’s Mars habitat in Houston on Saturday, Oct. 31, 2026. They had gone in on Oct. 19, 2025, and stayed 378 days with no windows and no live calls home.

Ellis, the crew’s medical officer, later told NASA that on most days she did not feel isolated. She said she relied on her three teammates every day to get through the mission.

The year inside was CHAPEA Mission 2, NASA’s second yearlong simulation of life on the surface of Mars. NASA posted the crew’s reflections on Nov. 9. The health data are still to be interpreted in two weeks of post-mission evaluations.

The habitat is called Mars Dune Alpha. It was 3D-printed, covers roughly 1,700 square feet (158 square meters) and sits inside a building at Johnson Space Center.

On entry day in October 2025, the crew rode to the habitat in the Space Exploration Vehicle, a prototype pressurized rover, according to NASA’s Human Research Program.

Suzanne Bell, the mission’s co-principal investigator, spoke to them at the door. Two alternate crew members posed with them for photos before the start.

CHAPEA is short for Crew Health and Performance Exploration Analog. The first mission used the same habitat and ended on July 6, 2024.

Before the second crew went in, the plans for a second yearlong rehearsal in the printed habitat put crew autonomy at the center.

NASA astronaut Jack Hathaway spoke about the time they were away. While they were inside, he flew a 237-day mission on the International Space Station and came back. The Artemis II crew also traveled around the Moon in that period and set a distance record for humans.

Hathaway said the year required “immense physiological and psychological strength, teamwork, and human grit.”

Messages between the habitat and mission control took up to 22 minutes each way. The crew spoke with their families only by email, with a Mars-like delay, NASA said in a one-year update on Oct. 19.

On May 7 the crew reached day 200. That week they were in a two-week loss of signal, a planned blackout like the one when Mars passes behind the Sun, according to a NASA update from May. They worked with no contact with mission control and used preplanned procedures.

The crew also had to handle simulated equipment failures and limited supplies. Michael Stenger, chief scientist of the Human Research Program, said the mission was starting to show NASA what year-long missions need, including medical and psychological care across communication delays.

Elder was the commander, Ellis the medical officer, Montgomery the science officer and Spicer the flight engineer. As part of their personal item allowance, the four brought musical instruments into the habitat.

Elder is a former U.S. Air Force experimental test pilot. According to NASA, he found the Marswalks and robotic work very similar to working as part of a flight.

For a Marswalk, the crew put on suits, went through an airlock and walked on red sand outside the habitat. That area is still inside the building. From inside, crew members also ran a drone and a rover to survey the ground and find or collect rock samples.

Elder said that getting suits ready, doing airlock procedures and making long traverses took time and stamina. He said food, exercise and rest were needed to keep up that pace.

Montgomery helped identify the rocks brought back from the walks. One NASA photo shows Ellis and Elder doing a maintenance task outside during a simulated walk. Another shows photos the crew placed around a boot print in the sand.

Spicer worked in satellite communications and spacecraft development before the mission. At the exit, he was thanked for keeping the habitat’s systems running and was called the crew’s resident data scientist.

He compared the year to living on a submarine or a boat. The crew counted supplies from dish soap to respirator filters, and they rationed food and water.

Spicer said he was surprised by how little he missed the news and social media, even though the crew could get news. He told his crewmates they made the year “not a chore, but a joy.”

Montgomery had worked in hardware engineering, robotics, tech startups and independent films. He set up the habitat’s 3D printing capabilities and ran a seed germination test with simulated Martian soil and a control.

He also grew vegetables in a hydroponic system. NASA has run other work on growing lettuce, peppers and tomatoes in microgravity, because packaged food can lose flavor and vitamins.

Ellis is a U.S. Space Force officer and has worked at the National Reconnaissance Office. Inside the habitat she helped organize biological sample collections and prepare supplies for the health studies.

She said it was hard to explain to other people what life inside was like. “It would be difficult to convince someone who has never lived in isolation that most days, I don’t feel isolated,” she said.

Ellis thanked the exercise, behavioral health, nutrition and medical specialists on the ground. She said the main lesson for her was that a Mars crew will need a closely integrated ground team.

Isolation has also been studied at Concordia Station in Antarctica, where researchers linked living in close quarters to more conflict and mistrust.

The crew started two weeks of tests and debriefs with NASA scientists after the exit. Scientists will compare the data with what the crew reported. Mission 2’s results are still to come, and each crew has only four people.

Grace Douglas, the principal investigator, said the results will help NASA weigh health risks against crew time, communication capacity and food and water systems. She also said many of the resource problems in space are like problems on Earth.

Sara Whiting, the project scientist, said the data will feed into plans for NASA’s Moon Base near the lunar South Pole. “This is really the now moment for this kind of data,” she said.

NASA’s live broadcast of the CHAPEA Mission 2 crew leaving the Mars Dune Alpha habitat on Oct. 31, 2026. Credit: NASA Johnson.

On Oct. 31, the four stepped outside for the first time in 378 days, where family, friends and colleagues met them. Ellis had said that on most days inside, she did not feel isolated.

Litecoin remains one of the most reliable payment-focused cryptocurrencies, known for fast transactions and low fees. As the market shifts toward practical use cases, LTC continues to hold relevance, especially in stable, utility-driven environments. At the same time, price action is starting to stabilize.

Instead of trending lower, LTC is now forming a base, with volatility tightening and selling pressure fading. Such phases often precede stronger moves once momentum returns. In this Litecoin price prediction, we examine whether this stability can translate into a breakout phase in 2026.

Litecoin is facing renewed selling pressure after breaking below a rising support trendline, weakening its November recovery prospects. At around $44.48, LTC is trading beneath its key moving averages, clustered near $51–$56, suggesting sellers remain in control.

The breakdown below the $50–$51 trendline support has shifted the short-term structure bearish. LTC now faces immediate support around $41–$42, where buyers previously stepped in. A sustained move below this zone could expose the $38–$40 range.

On the upside, $50–$51 is the first recovery hurdle, followed by stronger resistance around $55–$56. A breakout above these levels could bring $60–$63 back into focus.

Litecoin’s price prediction for 2026 points to a measured recovery phase rather than an aggressive breakout cycle, with price action largely dependent on its ability to reclaim key resistance levels. At present, LTC continues to trade within a well-defined range, with $50 acting as a strong demand floor and repeated rejections near the $60–$65 zone highlighting persistent supply. This structure indicates that while downside pressure has eased, bullish momentum remains constrained.

A sustained move above $50–$60 would be the first indication of structural improvement, as it would break the sequence of lower highs and allow Litecoin to advance toward the $80–$100 range. If this zone is reclaimed and held, the market could gradually expand toward $80–$120, aligning with previous distribution levels. However, failure to overcome this resistance band is likely to keep LTC in a range-bound environment, with intermittent rallies being capped and price rotating between established levels.

On the downside, a loss of the $50 support zone would expose Litecoin to a deeper pullback toward $44–$46, which represents the next significant demand area. That said, current price behavior suggests that buyers are actively defending this region, limiting the probability of extended downside unless broader market conditions weaken.

Overall, 2026 is expected to act as a rebuilding year for Litecoin, where the asset stabilizes, absorbs overhead supply, and gradually attempts to transition into a stronger trend.

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Il Milan continua a spinfere per Endrick, ma sulla strada dei rossoneri c'è un ostacolo enorme: José Mourinho. Stando a quanto riportato dai colleghi de Il Corriere dello Sport, il tecnico del Real Madrid considera il brasiliano un talento da valorizzare e non avrebbe alcuna intenzione di lasciarlo partire a gennaio, soprattutto a titolo definitivo. La posizione dello Special One è netta, e almeno per ora sembra pesare più dell'offerta economica che il club rossonero preparerebbe.

Il Milan avrebbe messo sul tavolo una proposta importante: 60 milioni di euro, con la possibilità di aggiungerne altri 10 attraverso bonus, oltre a una clausola di recompra da 80 milioni in favore del Real Madrid. Una formula pensata per convincere Florentino Perez e permettere ai Blancos di mantenere comunque il controllo sul giocatore.

Mourinho, però, considera Endrick una risorsa centrale per il progetto tecnico. Il minutaggio ridotto accumulato finora non avrebbe cambiato il suo giudizio: le parte sono molte e, nella sua visione, il brasiliano avrà spazio per crescere e diventare sempre più importante. Anche la Roma avrebbe manifestato interesse per un prestito, ma il muro del tecnico riguarda qualsiasi possibile partenza.

I rossoneri non sembrano comunque intenzionati ad arrendersi. Una possibile leva può arrivare da Brett Yormark, nuovo CEO del Milan Sports & Entertainment. Yormark è il fratello gemello di Michael Yormark, presidente di Roc Nations Sports International, l'agenzia che cura gli interessi di Endrick.

Il rapporto familiare e professionale tra i due, sommato ai legami già esistenti tra Gerry Cardinale e Roc Nation, potrebbe facilitarne il dialogo con l'entourage del giocatore. Resta però un problema decisivo: la volontà di Mourinho. Se il tecnico continuerà a considerare Endrick intoccabile, anche un'offerta da 60 milioni e una rete di relazioni privilegiata potrebbero non bastare.

Scotland face a mountainous task - but rewards are worth the climb

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

To emphasis the scale of what faces Scotland at the Sitterstadion in St Gallen on Monday night, the national team is looking to repeat a feat only carried out by Germany and Spain over the past two years.

Switzerland don’t lose often on home soil under Murat Yakin. The Germans and the world champions have prevailed in the Alps that period. In short, it takes a very good performance to leave with the full loot. And for Scotland to stand a chance in achieving their objective and finishing second in Nations League Group B1, they need to win.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Even then, it might not be enough. Sitting third on seven points going into Monday’s final round of matches, Scotland trail second-placed Slovenia by a point. Should the Slovenians defeat bottom-of-the-table North Macedonia in Skopje, the Scots could win 5-0 and it wouldn’t change the standings.

A draw won’t help either, as Slovenia have a better head-to-head record over Scotland by virtue of their 2-1 win at Hampden last month.

Ending up second is important. It would move Scotland into a play-off spot and a chance of promotion to Nations League A. It would also ensure a second seeding for the Euro 2028 qualification draw next month. Scotland are a host nation for the tournament, and getting in through the back door of a reserved place for non-qualifiers would be embarrassing - although not as humiliating as not being there at all.

The odds of toppling Slovenia, however, are stacked against Scotland. It requires two surprise results. North Macedonia have been meek throughout this campaign and have no points to show for their name, even if their display at Hampden on Friday showed glimmers of improvement in a 2-1 loss. The Scots need a favour in the Balkans.

Switzerland may give Scotland head coach Sebastien Pocognoli some optimism. Yakin is planning to shuffle his pack after landing top spot with a game to spare. Some of the star Swiss names may be kept in reserve. Granit Xhaka, Manuel Akanji, Dan Ndoye and Johan Manzambi are definitely out.

Nevertheless, Yakin vowed that his team will still be strong,. “It has happened a few times already. But we will make sure we get good level of player onto the pitch.

“It’s good thing we have won the group already. It’s now an opportunity to make changes. It doesn’t make sense to wait for injuries players. We have enough opportunities and possibilities.

“We will rotate a but but we first have to make sure that we have a stable defence with experienced defenders. Of course, we will try to get as many players involved as possible. There are a lot of players who haven’t played at all, so they can play from the start.

“We want our boys to get a chance and look forward to it and show their best game. We will certainly make some changes in attack.”

It is of benefit to Scotland that they face an opponent with nothing on the line. Especially as there are areas of concern over how the team has fared so far under Pocognoli’s watch.

The game against Switzerland will be Pocognoli’s sixth in charge after two wins against North Macedonia, two defeats (Switzerland and Slovenia at home) and a draw in Ljubljana. The performance on Friday was far from convincing, but for Pocognoli to get his first win at Hampden should have lifted a bit of weight from the 39-year-old Belgian’s shoulders.

Pocognoli stuck with a back three and Ryan Porteous, Luke Graham and Kieran Tierney coped pretty well with North Macedonia’s blunt attack. The visitors’ goal came from a set-piece and they didn’t look good enough to score in open play, even if they had attacking moments.

The worry for many Scotland fans is that better teams will punish Scotland when they either counter-attack or break through the press. Pocognoli’s insistence on playing a high line and asking his team to be proactive most of the time means that the defence does get exposed. And the top teams will take advantage.

Shorn of many defenders, it is likely Pocognoli will stick with the same back three. There is clamour for a back four, but the head coach will surely be reluctant to throw away all of his work thus far. His biggest conundrum is whether to keep winger Ben Gannon-Doak as his right wing-back or deploy the more rounded and defensively-savvy Aaron Hickey there.

On the other flank, a massive piece of history will be made. Captain Andy Robertson will win his 103rd cap and become Scotland’s most-capped male player of all time. It is an incredible achievement for the 32-year-old, who has been front and centre of Scotland’s recent renaissance and while he will try to shrug off the limelight, this night does belong to him.

Robertson will not want such a milestone tarnished by a defeat and neither will his team-mates. “It just takes time to click but it’s up to us as players now to show we can do what he wants,” said striker Che Adams of Pocognoli’s philosophy. “We have been together for a number of years now so we know each other’s game. You could see on Friday us starting to put the manager’s things in practice, we won a lot of balls high with our pressing.

“Coming off a transitional period like we have just now, it’s difficult to know where you are and what you want to do. But all the boys here want to play for our country and do Scotland proud.”

A win in Switzerland would do the nation proud. Depending on what happens 990 miles down the road in Skopje, it may or may not be enough to finish second in the group, but a positive display would give Scotland something to build upon next year when the Euro 2028 qualifiers start and give a sceptical public reasons for optimism.

10/10 RPG Drops On Steam, & You Can Try It Free Now

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A new RPG has dropped on Steam that's already receiving positive praise, and you can try it for free right now. There are many new games being published on Steam every day, and most of them get lost in the thousands of titles housed on the platform. Once in a while, a 10/10 gem will rise up out of the slop, and this new title is one of these winners.

This new title has it all: an intriguing story, striking art style, and a dark atmosphere that shrouds the whole thing in existential dread. The reviews are in, and one of Steam's newest hidden gems is an Overwhelmingly Positive treasure that more gamers should know about. And best of all, it has a demo available to download and play for free.

Annihilation was released on November 10 on Steam and has already amassed an Overwhelmingly Positive rating, with 96% (or 1,128) reviews finding it a well-crafted and unique experience. It's a great start for developer Death Kumiai, a small Japanese studio for whom Annihilation is a debut title.

The game combines deck-builder roguelite elements with a strikingly illustrated visual novel. The story is at the heart of this game's success. It asks what happens when a hero is stripped from the very thing that made them special.

You play as the only survivor of a rescue party sent to save a party of heroes lost in the depths of a cursed dungeon. When you arrive, you find the four party members, but all are broken: the hero has lost their ability to speak, the mage lost their magic, the warior lost their body (and got turned into a life-sized doll), and the priest lost, well... something.

You can only rescue one hero, so you choose one and head back up through the treacherous dungeon, leaving the rest behind. Adventure toward safety with your broken hero companion by your side, and, as you do, learn more about them and what happened to them.

Each hero in Annihilation has a different skillset and story, which revolve around strategic deck-building that relies on your companion's special abilities. You can choose from timing-based and probability-based combat, depending on which you prefer. All the cards in your hand break after use, forcing you to think on your feet and constantly adjust your strategy to suit the cards at your disposal.

Between battles, you'll have a chance to speak and interact with the hero, and how these conversations go will affect how the story plays out. There are four routes—one for each hero you can partner up with—and each route has multiple endings depending on the route you take and conversations you have.

The game has over 400 hand-drawn illustrations, more than 100 weapons and items to collect, and over 200,000 words of dialogue in the vast branching story. It's a breath of fresh air for fans of both deckbuilders and story-driven indie games.

Annihilation is available on Steam for $16.99, and a demo is available to play for free. The title is on sale at 10% through November 23, which means you can get it for just $15.29 for a limited time.

Charlton Athletic have landed a £500k masterstroke - Blackburn Rovers will be shocked

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Charlton Athletic may not be as defensively strong as a collective as Nathan Jones would like through the opening 15 games of the campaign, but the individual performances of new arrival Billy Koumetio have been very promising.

The Addicks ended an 11-game winless run with a 2-1 victory over Sheffield United in the final game before the international break. The three points helped open up a seven-point gap between themselves and the bottom three, and helped alleviate some of the pressure on Jones.

Ultimately, the goal at the Valley this season will be like the one last year, too: stay up and continue to establish themselves as a Championship side once more. The last time that Charlton spent three or more straight seasons at this level was between 2012 and 2016.

They don't have the budget that the majority of other sides in the division do, so they're relying on players performing above their level and some bargain hidden gem arrivals to get by. Koumetio falls into the latter, and he's shown promising signs so far this season.

Koumetio is a former French Under-20 international. He spent his entire English youth career with Liverpool, but hasn't been able to make strides in the senior game.

Before he signed for Charlton this summer, he spent two years at Dundee following his departure from the Premier League side. However, the defender struggled to make his mark in Scottish football.

Koumetio was initially a key part of Tony Docherty's side, but after picking up an ankle injury in December 2024, he struggled to get back into contention upon his return the following April.

Dundee changed managers in the summer of 2025, with current boss Steven Pressley taking the reins, and Koumetio only played 16 league games last season, along with a couple of starts in the relegation round.

It was this lack of usage that saw him be sold for just £500,000 to Charlton this past summer, but he's finally finding his feet. He's played every minute he's been available for, starting just one league game fewer than he did in the entirety of last season.

Koumetio has finally started to settle somewhere, and it's reflecting well in his stats.

As mentioned, barring a near two-month layoff after picking up an injury against Tottenham Hotspur in the EFL Cup, Koumetio has played every minute for his new club, and the defender has been well worth his place in Nathan Jones' side.

A recent chart detailing the number of completed progressive passes per 90 minutes and the percentage of aerial duels won this season amongst Championship defenders has Koumetio sitting as one of the best in both.

Football League World has asked AI to rank the top nine toughest fanbases in the EFL Championship right now from least to most

Only West Ham United's Konstantinos Mavropanos and Portsmouth's Regan Poole have won a higher percentage of their aerial duels while posting above-average progressive passing metrics this season.

And for the defensive traditionalists out there, Koumetio is still above average when it comes to defending his box, too. He records 11.33 defensive contributions per 90, sitting in the top 12% of centre-backs in the division, and posts eight clearances per game and 1.44 blocked shots per game, too, per FotMob.

For £500,000, Koumetio is proving to be a steal and is going under the radar mainly due to generally poor defensive numbers from Charlton as a group. Evidently, they'd be much worse without him.

Going back to Koumetio failing to find his feet and cement himself as an important player somewhere, that wasn't just attributed to the two years he spent in Dundee. His loan spells from Liverpool weren't productive either.

He made just 11 appearances for Austria Wien in 2022, nine appearances for French second-tier side Dunkerque in the first half of the 2023/24 campaign, and in the second half of that same year, joined Blackburn Rovers.

The 2023/24 term saw Blackburn struggling at the bottom of the Championship. Jon Dahl Tomasson stated after the January window closed that the club was in a "very serious situation," and that he'd even offered to leave the previous summer after cuts in the playing budget.

Collect every point to win the round. Don’t let Neil Warnock catch you.

Arrow keys or WASD to move. P to pause, M to mute.

Swipe on the pitch or use the pad to move.

For a then-22-year-old who hadn't had much success with loans elsewhere, Ewood Park wasn't exactly the best place to turn that round. Furthermore, Tomasson left just days after the window closed, so the Frenchman had to play under a boss who didn't bring him to the club.

In the end, Koumetio was massively unfavoured by John Eustace. His only appearance came off the bench in their FA Cup exit against Newcastle United. Despite featuring in 12 Championship squads, he never made a league bow for Blackburn.

To see the defender now excelling in this division just two years later must be rather surprising for those at Ewood Park, and a twinge of envy may be forming, too.

Charlton don't really have too many saleable assets or players that clubs further up the pyramid or abroad would look to spend decent seven-figure, or perhaps even eight-figure fees for.

However, if Koumetio can keep this personal performance level up, and Nathan Jones' side starts to post better defensive numbers as a collective, the Frenchman will definitely be looked at as someone the Addicks could make some decent profit on.

Do you think Billy Koumetio could be worth a decent fee in the future? Let us know in the comments.

The centre-back is still relatively young. He's 23 as of writing, but could be 24 by the time this is released, and his progressive passing stats lend well to how the game is played right now.

That, plus some aerial dominance and general solidity in the more 'traditional' defensive stats, means that the sky could be the limit for someone like Koumetio, and the defender, who has plenty of potential given his days in the Liverpool academy, could make that step up.

If he does become an asset for Charlton, Blackburn will definitely be looking on, wondering why they weren't able to get a similar tune out of him.

The Acer Predator Atlas 7 is my favorite handheld of 2026 and it cures the memory pricing crisis in style

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Acer Predator Atlas 7 sticks a middle finger up to RAMageddon and strikes the right balance of price, raw performance and efficient software — overcoming a few ergonomic quirks and a smaller battery to become my favorite handheld of 2026.

Significantly cheaper than Z2 Extreme competition

Included matte screen protector fixes glossy display reflections

Display’s not the brightest or most colorful

Dual-fan cooling system gets loud under load

Shallow grips have never been my cup of tea

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

Handheld gaming has become one of the most exciting, yet terrifying, categories in tech. It feels great to have your PC library with you everywhere, but to get there, you either grab something that’s underpowered and overpriced, or you skip rent for a month to get something actually good.

The Acer Predator Atlas 7 is the rare exception to that rule.

At £900 in the U.K. (not available in the U.S. in this base configuration yet), it is a rather unassuming slab of a handheld that hides a monster inside: an Intel Arc G3 chip paired with 16GB of LPDDR5X memory.

It easily outmuscles more expensive AMD Ryzen Z2 Extreme competitors while dodging massive component price hikes. And while you will have to manage that smaller 60Wh battery and some loud fans, the sheer gaming grunt for that price makes it my undeniable handheld of the year.

7-inch HD (1920 x 1080) LCD 120Hz refresh rate

1x Micro SD, 2x Thunderbolt 4, 1x 3.5mm audio jack

So as I started taking this on solo trips to the pub (yep, I’m that guy) and on my travels all over the place, I started to really see the strengths in the Atlas 7.

The real superpower is what’s inside: the highly efficient Intel Arc G3 equipped with a B370 GPU and 16GB of LPDDR5X memory. Up until this point, I’d only tested the Arc G3 Extreme, so I was a little nervous heading into its smaller, weaker sibling.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Asus ROG Xbox Ally X20 (AMD Ryzen Z2 AI Extreme)

The Arc G3 gives you enough breathing room to push your 1080p settings, and sits comfortably above the capabilities of the Ryzen Z2 Extreme in the ROG Xbox Ally X20 — a handheld that’s a third more expensive.

Plus, when you slap Intel’s XeSS and multi-frame generation on top of a solid baseline frame rate, it feels properly zippy and responsive.

On a compact 7-inch screen, you don’t see the ghosting or sizzling artifacts that you would on a bigger desktop monitor with the Arc B580 GPU.

Asus ROG Xbox Ally X (AMD Ryzen Z2 AI Extreme)

There is an anomaly in the Black Myth: Wukong benchmark, but for every other game it was all Intel domination. I think that may be down to optimization for particular silicon.

On top of that, it is also an extremely capable docked system; Star Wars Galactic Racer ran incredibly smooth at 1440p on medium settings with XeSS set to Performance mode.

And don’t get me wrong. You’ll get much more from the Extreme variant, but you’re paying much more to take it up a notch. With the Arc, this much power at this price is right on the money.

Acer nailed the inputs here. They opted for TMR joysticks, which are incredibly smooth and highly reliable since there are no internal gears grinding away. The face buttons offer a satisfying, tactile clickiness, and while the triggers have a decent amount of depth, I do wish they had a slightly more defined flick to catch my fingers.

That being said though, this is a nice lightweight system that sees its mass evenly distributed for level playing.

Windows 11 has finally improved significantly for handhelds. This has been the result of months of work sharpening up the setup process — no more nagging screens asking if you want an Office 365 subscription, just a quick login, some updates and you are ready to play.

For hardware tweaking, that’s where Acer’s PredatorSense overlay comes in. Pressing the top left button triggers it, and while I’d say Asus’ ROG overlay has a slightly nicer UI, PredatorSense puts all the essential customization settings right within a thumb’s reach without overcomplicating things.

But to get something together as a value for money proposition, you have to make some compromises.

The most obvious one is the battery. It’s still sort of decent longevity wise, especially if you get into dropping the TDP to its lowest possible setting and cranking out some indie games. But in our lab test, it’s not great.

This surprised me given the effectiveness of those efficiency cores on the MSI Claw 8 EX AI+ and its Arc G3 Extreme. But no amount of efficient silicon can overcome a smaller cell.

Out of the box, the Atlas 7 shares similar ergonomics with the old ROG Ally and Steam Deck, featuring shallower grips that aren’t as ergonomically sound as the chunkier grips on the Ally X20. The end result is a system you’d lean on a table more than comfortably suspended in the air.

Thermally, the dual-fan cooling system delivers impressive sustained performance, keeping the back of the device nice and cool even at peak output. But the trade-off is noise; those fans do get loud when the system is fully unleashed.

And finally, the 120Hz display has color and is visible indoors, but the brightness isn’t quite there for playing by a window/strong light source. Plus the glossy finish turns that screen into a mirror, though Acer thankfully includes a matte screen protector in the box.

The Acer Predator Atlas 7 leaves me conflicted, but in the best way possible.

For all intents and purposes, this is the best handheld of the year. Yes, there are devices with better displays, chunkier ergonomics and better battery life. But in terms of striking a strong enough mix across all metrics, Acer’s nailed it.

At £900, it is significantly cheaper than the competition it beats, but I also admit that it's hard to call almost a grand “cheap.” That being said, in the midst of a component crisis where competing devices approach the £2,000 mark with worse performance, the Atlas 7 actually makes you feel like you’re getting your money’s worth.

Jason brings a decade of tech and gaming journalism experience to his role as a Managing Editor of Computing at Tom's Guide. He has previously written for Laptop Mag, Tom's Hardware, Kotaku, Stuff and BBC Science Focus. In his spare time, you'll find Jason looking for good dogs to pet or thinking about eating pizza if he isn't already.

Helsing backer Plural raises $1.1B to fund Europe’s next moonshots

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Operator-led VC firm Plural has raised €1 billion ($1.1 billion) in new capital, joining a growing cohort of larger European funds that also includes Mundi Ventures’ Kembara and EQT-managed Scaleup Europe Fund.

Despite its size, Plural’s fundraise isn’t (only) for a growth fund: two thirds will go to its third early-stage fund, and one third to a new “opportunities” fund making select follow-on investments, TechCrunch learned from Plural partners Khaled Helioui and Carina Namih.

The two buckets are fixed, but the check size is not. Plural is “still very committed to being an early stage fund,” Namih said. But Helioui added that some of the early-stage “moonshots” it plans to back may require “raising a couple hundred million” to get off the ground.

“Moonshot” is an appropriate term to describe some of Plural’s portfolio highlights, such as Proxima Fusion and The Exploration Company — and its new fund will be looking for equally promising opportunities in energy, AI infrastructure, defense, health, and more.

Many of these are capital-intensive, but this is no longer stopping European entrepreneurs, especially second-time founders, and companies like Daniel Ek’s Helsing are “not isolated cases,” said Helioui, who backed the startup’s seed round alongside two Plural partners.

“If you’ve built and scaled a company, chances are, you’re more likely to be driven and passionate about having an impact on Europe’s ability to defend itself, procure its own energy, or own its operations and manufacturing, rather than just another app,” he said.

With bigger pockets, Plural will be able to keep up longer with the larger and larger rounds its portfolio companies have gone on to raise. It also made an effort to raise more of this capital from Europe, Namih said.

While Europe’s institutional LPs were, for a long time, more likely to back banker types than founders hoping to displace those bankers, the current shift towards Europe’s sovereignty and resilience creates tailwinds for Plural, whose office in Tallinn played a key role in its decision to invest in defense tech — including in a missile company, Hypersonica.

“The reality is today still, you have a different level of conversation and a different sense of appreciation of the urgency and the criticality of these topics depending on how far from the border to Russia you are,” said Helioui.

Two of Plural’s six partners are Estonians, and Skype alumni: Wise cofounder Taavet Hinrikus and Teleport’s Sten Tamkivi. However, there is more to Plural than Tallinn; the fund also has offices in London, where Helioui, Namih and Ian Hogarth are based; and in Paris, where it hired former Uber executive Pierre-Dimitri Gore-Coty as a full-time partner in October 2025.

According to a press release, each partner will lead a small number of new investments a year. These companies will then get access to Plural’s growing portfolio support, which already ranges from hiring and communications to finance, Helioui said. That’s another indicator that VC firms in Europe are increasingly starting to look like their U.S. peers.

Pictured from left to right: Ian Hogarth, Taavet Hinrikus, Khaled Helioui, Carina Namih, Sten Tamkivi, and Pierre-Dimitri Gore-Coty.
