We Built an MCP Execution Platform for AI Agents. Here Are 10 Things We Learned.

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Giving an AI agent a shell is easy. Giving it a computer it can actually work with is a different problem.

When we started building WBX Sandbox, the idea was simple: give AI agents a real Linux environment where they can execute code safely. A shell, a filesystem, network access when needed, and a way to get results back.

We quickly discovered that running code was the easy part. The difficult questions came later:

Most of what we built since then came from getting those wrong first. Today, WBX runs isolated Linux microVMs using Firecracker on our own infrastructure in the EU. Agents can access them through MCP, REST, a Python SDK, or a LangChain integration.

Here are ten things we learned along the way, including a few mistakes.

Our first requirement was straightforward: an AI agent should be able to run real code. Not simulated code execution, not a restricted expression evaluator, but an actual Linux environment.

Every WBX sandbox runs in an isolated Firecracker microVM with its own kernel, filesystem, and network configuration. The agent can install packages, run tests, automate a browser, or launch a server and expose it on a public HTTPS URL.

Early on, every task started from an empty machine, and agents spent a lot of time just installing things. So we added ready-to-use templates for Python, Node.js, Go, Rust, browser automation, and other common workloads. We also use warm pools, snapshots, and verified dependency caches to reduce setup overhead.

Lesson: Isolation matters, but developer experience starts with how quickly useful work can begin.

Imagine asking an agent to run a large test suite that takes 25 minutes. If the work is tied to one MCP tool request, a timeout or disconnected client becomes a serious problem.

We first tied everything to the tool request and kept hitting timeouts. The fix was to separate interactive execution from background jobs. For quick commands, the agent uses tools such as run_command. For longer work, it uses run_job.

The job keeps running after the client disconnects, and the agent can return later and retrieve the results.

Lesson: The lifetime of a computation should not depend on the lifetime of a chat connection.

One of the less obvious problems we encountered was polling. An agent starts a background job and then repeatedly asks:

Every call consumes tool overhead, context, and time. Instead, we implemented wait_jobs: the server waits for a job or batch to finish, or until a specified timeout, and the agent receives a result when something meaningful happens.

We also added get_job_summary, which returns a compact overview instead of forcing the model to read every detail, and list_jobs, so an agent can find earlier jobs and batches without having to remember their IDs.

Lesson: Move waiting and state management into the platform. Don't make the language model do repetitive coordination work.

Developers are used to scrolling through thousands of log lines. AI agents don't have that luxury. A large build or failing test suite can generate megabytes of output, and if you send everything back to the model, you're spending context on information that may not matter.

So we built log summaries that recognize common test frameworks, extract error messages with their surrounding context, and preserve the end of the log. The original logs remain available when deeper inspection is necessary.

A similar principle applies to datasets. In one experiment, we processed a CSV file containing 100,000 orders. Measured with common tokenizers:

It's one experiment, not a general benchmark, but it shows why keeping data processing outside the context window matters.

Lesson: Return the information the agent needs, not every byte the computer produced.

A microVM is the right tool when an agent needs a full Linux system. But many tasks are small, self-contained computations: parse a file, transform data, run a function.

For those we added a WebAssembly (WASI) runtime. Modules run without starting a VM, as unprivileged processes with no network, and see only /input, /output, and /tmp. They start almost instantly and have strict memory and time limits.

The agent uses the same job interface for both, so it doesn't have to learn a second system.

Lesson: Match the isolation to the task. A lighter sandbox for small work keeps the heavy one available for real environments.

AI workflows often involve independent tasks: testing different configurations, processing multiple datasets, running build matrices, evaluating several implementations.

We added run_batch to handle up to 1,000 jobs with configurable concurrency. Jobs can depend on one another and pass result files to downstream tasks.

As a sanity check we started 65 sandboxes across nine templates at the same time and let each run a five-minute task. All of them finished. It was a test of concurrency and lifecycle handling with mostly idle processes, not a performance benchmark.

Lesson: Good orchestration lets agents work in parallel without making the model manually coordinate every machine.

Suppose an agent requests an IPv6-only environment, or a higher-performance compute class. If the platform cannot provide it, quietly substituting something else is dangerous. The task might still run, but its results may no longer mean what the developer expects.

We decided that requested capabilities must be explicit. If the right environment is unavailable, the request waits or fails clearly.

WBX supports IPv4, dual-stack, and IPv6-only networking, and IPv6 sandboxes get their own public address for outgoing traffic. Incoming connections from the internet are closed for now; web apps get an HTTPS URL instead. In a live connectivity test, we verified public IPv6 allocation, IPv6 routing, successful ICMP ping and HTTPS requests. We also confirmed that ordinary IPv4 outbound connections were blocked in the IPv6-only mode.

Lesson: A clear failure is better than a successful execution in the wrong environment.

A fresh sandbox is useful for a one-off task. It is much less useful when an agent is building a project over several days. Imagine spending hours installing dependencies, creating files, and configuring a development environment, and then the chat ends. Starting from zero next time is frustrating.

We added sandbox sleeping to preserve the disk while the VM is not running, and named sandboxes allow a later conversation to locate the same environment. We also built archive storage, so sleeping disks are moved to dedicated storage nodes and restored later on any compute node.

This introduced difficult engineering questions around crash recovery, metadata consistency, replication, and disk failure. Preserving files isn't just about successfully writing them once. It's about being able to recover them when something goes wrong.

Lesson: Persistence changes the reliability expectations of an execution platform. Treat user data as something you must protect, not disposable temporary output.

An AI assistant might say: "I ran the tests. Everything passed." But how can the user verify that?

We added execution receipts. A completed job records information such as its runtime, exit code, resource usage, network policy, and output file hashes. It's an audit record, not a cryptographic attestation, but it provides a concrete record of execution independent of the assistant's summary.

Lesson: Agents should be able to show evidence of completed work, not just describe what they believe happened.

This took us the longest to understand. When you're building a normal developer API, you write documentation for a person. When you're building an MCP server, the model itself often decides which tool to call. That makes tool descriptions part of the actual user experience.

We also added MCP tool annotations such as readOnlyHint, destructiveHint, and openWorldHint. Better descriptions help models choose the right operation without repeatedly exploring the API.

Lesson: When AI models use your API, good tool descriptions become operational infrastructure.

If we were starting again, we'd prioritize these things much earlier:

We also learned that the difficult bugs are rarely in the happy path. They're in the moments when a node disappears, a response gets lost, a disk runs out of space, or a process crashes between two writes. Those are the moments that determine whether an execution platform can be trusted.

AI agents are becoming capable software operators, and the more capable they become, the more they need dependable infrastructure around them. Not just a shell, but isolation, background execution, persistent files, useful logs, explicit capabilities, and evidence of completed work.

That's what we're trying to get right with WBX Sandbox, and we're still learning.

If you're building infrastructure for AI agents, I'd really like to hear how you handle long-running tasks and persistent execution environments.

Project: https://sandbox.wbx.cz MCP endpoint: https://sandbox.wbx.cz/mcp

Disclosure: I'm building WBX Sandbox. This post shares engineering lessons from developing the platform, including features and failure scenarios we've been working through.

Qualche polemica, ma la linea Orsato piace ai club: più gioco, meno falli. I tre arbitri migliori

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Il Var continua a lasciare dubbi: alle volte sembra una lampadina fulminata, s'accende e si spegne senza che sia chiaro perché. Però, la prima parte di stagione arbitrale va in archivio con un bilancio complessivamente positivo per Daniele Orsato. Il nuovo designatore, sulla scorta delle indicazioni ricevute dalla UEFA, ha imposto una linea precisa, che sta dando risultati soprattutto sul piano della fluidità del gioco. E sotto sotto piace un po' a tutti i club, che a parte qualche polemica gradiscono i numeri: dopo undici giornate, la media dei falli fischiati in Serie A è scesa da 25,3 a 22 per partita, con una riduzione del 13%. Diminuiscono anche i rigori, 24 in 110 gare, per una media di 0,22 rispetto allo 0,28 del campionato precedente. Il dato sul tempo effettivo, fa si avvicina all'ora, con un recupero di circa sei minuti rispetto allo stesso periodo del 2025/26. Merito anche delle nuove regole contro le perdite di tempo, ma soprattutto sui contatti la differenza rispetto al passato è evidente.

Non significa che siano mancate le polemiche. Già a settembre Orsato aveva riconosciuto cinque errori arbitrali in Serie A, tra cui i rigori non concessi in Torino-Milan, Fiorentina-Frosinone e Juventus-Atalanta. Nelle settimane successive, il caso più discusso è stato quello di Como-Venezia, con il rigore inizialmente assegnato da Ferrieri Caputi e poi revocato dopo l'intervento del VAR: una revisione che non ha convinto neppure il designatore, presente in tribuna. Più recentemente, le proteste della Juventus per il mancato rigore sul tocco di mano di Joao Mario in Fiorentina-Juventus, con Colombo arbitro e Di Paolo al VAR. La linea del maggiore coinvolgimento dell'arbitro di campo funziona, ma alcuni episodi hanno evidenziato la necessità di trovare maggiore uniformità nell'applicazione del protocollo e lo sa lo stesso Orsato, che non è un nemico del Var: vuole solo che intervenga quando serva.

Nel bilancio individuale meritano una menzione particolare Valerio Crezzini, Juan Luca Sacchi ed Ermanno Feliciani. Il primo è probabilmente la rivelazione più interessante: quattro partite dirette, appena tre ammonizioni, nessuna espulsione e nessun rigore assegnato. In Atalanta-Parma ha chiuso addirittura senza estrarre cartellini: personalità. Sacchi ha garantito continuità di rendimento nelle cinque direzioni stagionali, con dodici ammonizioni e nessun espulso, distinguendosi anche nella gestione di partite importanti come Milan-Bologna e Venezia-Napoli. Bene anche Feliciani, che nelle quattro gare arbitrate ha mostrato un metro coerente con le indicazioni del designatore, limitando i provvedimenti disciplinari a sette ammonizioni. Tre nomi che, pur con caratteristiche ed esperienza differenti, rappresentano finora alcune delle interpretazioni più convincenti del nuovo corso arbitrale.

Should You Invest Now or Wait Until 2027? Here's What a Century of History Says.

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Waiting until the new year to reset comes with some drawbacks.

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.40%) last hit a closing record high on Oct. 6. Since then, it lost 4.4% in a single day and was as much as 6% below its all-time high. Even now, the index is roughly 3% below its October peak.

Volatility has picked up, investors have been rotating into non-tech equities, and there are certainly some potential vulnerabilities in the U.S. stock market heading into 2027. It would be understandable if someone were compelled to sit on the sidelines and wait for some of the signals to clear. But history says that investors who wait tend to come out behind.

Over the past century, the S&P 500 has returned around 10% annually. That number includes every recession, bear market, and major economic event along the way. The key, however, is remaining invested throughout these periods.

Charles Schwab (SCHW +1.73%) did a study recently about hypothetical investors who invested their money regularly along with folks who invested at market tops and bottoms. It found that people who invested entirely at the beginning of every calendar or monthly throughout the year actually didn't outperform a person who had perfect market timing (which, of course, is impossible). Even the person who invested at the market top every year still produced solid returns over the long term.

The only person who really fell behind was the one who consistently sat on the sidelines.

The lesson is that even if you consistently invest at the worst possible time, being in the market is still better than being out of it over the long term.

JPMorgan Asset Management (JPM +0.98%) did a study of forward-looking returns when investing at all-time highs versus all other days. It turns out that investing at all-time highs was actually better for long-term returns than when the market was below them.

This sounds counterintuitive. A lot of people think that upside is somehow limited when the S&P 500 is at record highs. But stocks are usually at record highs for a reason. Companies and the economy are likely performing well, and that often helps drive future returns.

The biggest takeaway is that no one knows how the stock market is going to perform at any given time. So there's little point in trying to predict it by market timing.

The goal of long-term investing is to slowly accumulate wealth over time. The best path for that is to simply invest regularly and consistently. Don't worry about investing at the "right" time. Just focus on investing, period.

Sometimes you'll buy at highs. Sometimes you'll buy at lows. But you'll be invested at all times, and that ends up being the biggest key to success of them all.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 823%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 209% for the S&P 500.

They just revealed what they believe are the 10 best stocks for investors to buy right now, available when you join Stock Advisor.

What Is Science, Really? Even Scientists Can’t Agree

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Even scientists in the same specialty disagree about science’s purpose, a survey finds, raising concerns that AI could automate one narrow approach at others’ expense.

Is science a search for objective truth, an exercise in creative problem-solving, or both? A study published in Royal Society Open Science found that scientists disagree about the meaning and purpose of science more than the general public does.

Study leader Marina Dubova, a former Santa Fe Institute postdoctoral researcher, is now an assistant professor of psychology at the University of California, Berkeley. Working with Princeton University researcher Qiawen E. Liu, she surveyed 91 scientists and 99 members of the public. Participants evaluated 18 historical descriptions of science, including science as detective work, a search for truth, and a mirror reflecting reality.

Scientists and the public generally agreed that science involves following curiosity and solving mysteries. The public tended to share an idealized picture of science as a reliable way to discover how the world works, placing less emphasis on creativity or uncertainty. That outlook could shape how people judge research and help explain their frustration when scientists cannot give firm answers or revise their conclusions as new evidence emerges.

The public’s views were closer to those of physicists and biologists than to those of researchers in psychology or the social sciences. One possible explanation, Dubova suggests, is that physics and biology feature more prominently in science education. More research is needed to test that connection.

Some scientists emphasized the search for objective truth, while others gave more weight to the judgment and creativity researchers bring to their work. They also disagreed about whether research follows established methods or requires improvisation. Few of the 18 descriptions received scientists’ full support, and they often had reservations even about ideas they generally agreed with.

“What was surprising to me was that there was quite a bit of disagreement between scientists, including scientists who are in the same subfield,” says Dubova. She suspects that researchers develop their approaches partly through experience, much as chefs develop different instincts about when to follow a recipe and when to improvise. Those differing conceptions of science could influence which discoveries researchers pursue.

That diversity also raises a question for efforts to automate scientific research. As researchers develop artificial intelligence systems capable of conducting science, whose understanding of the work will those systems embody? Dubova worries that automation could reinforce one narrow conception of science at the expense of other valuable approaches.

For Dubova, the study is an early step toward understanding how these outlooks shape research itself. “Don’t take the common understanding of science for granted,” she says. “Always ask. Always be curious about what someone has in mind.”

Reference: “Everyone knows what science is…until you ask” by Marina Dubova and Qiawen E. Liu, 11 November 2026, Royal Society Open Science. DOI: 10.1098/rsos.260933

The research was supported by SFI’s Lou Schuyler Seed Grant Fund, established in 2022 to support exploratory research by early-career scientists. Dubova used the funding to compensate study participants, helping make the research possible.

10 Best Selling Manga Of Summer 2026

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

BookWalker’s Summer 2026 manga chart offers an interesting snapshot of what readers were picking up between July 1 and September 30. Kaiju No. 8 took the top spot, followed by Shangri-La Frontier, while the rest of the top five included This Is Screwed Up, but I Was Reincarnated as a GIRL in Another World!, Smoking Behind the Supermarket with You, and The 100 Girlfriends Who Really, Really, Really, Really, Really Love You.

The rest of the chart showed the numbers 11 to 20 manga of summer 2026 as Granblue Fantasy; The Cursed Sword Master’s Harem Life: By the Sword, For the Sword; How to Deal When Your Intimidating Neighbor is Actually an Omega; Chained Soldier; The 100th Time’s the Charm: She Was Executed 99 Times, So How Did She Unlock “Super Love” Mode?!; Parallel Paradise; The Exiled Heavy Knight Knows How to Game the System (Omnibus); 2.5 Dimensional Seduction; The Fragrant Flower Blooms With Dignity; and Dorohedoro. This is a BookWalker-platform ranking, not a measure of all manga sales across retailers.

Naoya Matsumoto’s Kaiju No. 8 claimed first place, a notable result for a series that had already reached its finale. Its central character, Kafka Hibino, works cleaning up the remains left by giant monsters but has never abandoned his childhood ambition of joining Japan’s Defense Force. An unexpected transformation turns that dream into a dangerous contradiction.

These anime from Spring 2026 deserve a place among the best series of the year and are true gems that viewers won't regret giving a chance to.

The timing gave readers another reason to return, as VIZ released the 292-page English-language Volume 16 on September 1, 2026, marking the manga’s final volume. Kafka makes his last stand against the Daikaiju of the Meireki Era, bringing the long-running fight to its conclusion. That end point also offers newcomers a complete series to catch up on.

Shangri-La Frontier follows Rakuro Hizutome, a high school gamer who usually takes pride in conquering notoriously bad games. When he finally tries a celebrated virtual-reality title, he brings the same reckless confidence to a much bigger challenge. Playing as Sunraku, he enters the game wearing little more than shorts and a bird mask, then begins pushing its limits.

Ryosuke Fuji’s ongoing adventure ranked second, supported by a steady stream of new material. Kodansha’s Volume 26 arrived digitally on July 7, 2026, just after the ranking period began. Its story sends Sunraku and his allies into a dangerous clash with the serpent god, before shifting toward a rivalry between two powerful player clans.

Ashi’s isekai manga takes a different route through reincarnation. Its protagonist, Ren, was a male research scientist on Earth before waking in a fantasy world as an orphan girl. After being sold to a dangerous merchant, Ren escapes and draws on scientific knowledge from the previous life, alongside newly awakened magical abilities, to survive.

Volume 19 reached English-language readers on June 16, 2026, shortly before the chart’s July start. That installment puts Ren under pressure after a surprise attack leaves her afraid of men, despite having once been one. With help from friends Lillie and Arisa, she begins preparing a new invention: armor built around a golem.

Jinushi’s romantic slice-of-life Smoking Behind the Supermarket with You series centers on Sasaki, a 45-year-old salaryman worn down by office life. His small daily comfort is the cheerful supermarket cashier Yamada, but one evening he misses her shift and meets a sharp-tongued woman named Tayama in the smoking area. Sasaki does not realize the two women are the same person.

That gentle identity-based comedy helped the manga secure fourth place, and its seventh English volume arrived during the chart window. Released July 7, 2026, Volume 7 complicates Sasaki’s feelings as he grows suspicious about Tayama and Yamada. A troublesome customer also unsettles Yamada, while her sudden absence pushes Sasaki to confront the store’s growing crisis.

Rikito Nakamura and Yukiko Nozawa’s comedy begins with Aijo Rentaro, a student who has been rejected by 100 girls. After asking a god for help, he learns that a mistake has made him destined to meet 100 soulmates. The catch is severe: every soulmate must receive his love, or she faces a fatal accident.

2026 marks the end of an era and a shift in the manga landscape, with many long-running series bidding their final farewell in the coming months.

The series turns that outrageous setup into an escalating ensemble rom-com, with Rentaro trying to give every girlfriend meaningful attention rather than treating the premise as a simple gag. Seven Seas released Volume 18 on June 23, 2026, putting a fresh installment on shelves just before summer. Its fifth-place finish shows how strongly the sprawling cast continues to connect.

Grand Blue Dreaming brings college comedy to the seaside. After moving to the coastal town of Izu, freshman Iori Kitahara expects an ordinary start to university life, but his new surroundings pull him into the local diving club. The result mixes underwater excursions with chaotic parties, unruly friendships, awkward mishaps, and jokes that rarely stay restrained.

Written by Kenji Inoue and illustrated by Kimitake Yoshioka, the long-running series has a distinctive appeal beyond its outrageous humor. Diving gives the story a recurring sense of adventure, while Iori’s friendships keep it grounded in student life. Its sixth-place ranking suggests readers still enjoy returning to a manga where the beach is rarely peaceful.

This court fantasy follows Kou Reirin, a favored maiden whose future at the imperial court appears secure, and Shu Keigetsu, a rival treated as an outcast. Keigetsu uses forbidden magic to switch their bodies, leaving Reirin trapped in the form of the very woman who tried to ruin her standing.

The manga’s seventh-place finish coincided with the August 4, 2026 release of its ninth English volume. That installment brings the maidens into the Rite of Reverence, a competition that could influence their positions as future consorts. Reirin and Keigetsu must navigate court expectations while their complicated relationship continues to shift.

Kamome Shirahama’s Witch Hat Atelier manga follows Coco, a girl who dreams of becoming a witch despite being told that magic is only available to those born with the gift. Her life changes after she secretly witnesses the traveling magician Qifrey casting a spell and discovers that magic works differently from what she had been taught.

The series stands out for the way its intricate artwork supports the rules of its fantasy world. Coco learns to draw spells with special tools and ink, turning magic into a craft that requires study, precision, and responsibility. Its eighth-place result reflects the lasting appeal of a thoughtful fantasy adventure with a richly imagined visual language.

This fantasy comedy follows Ceres after the Hero, Zect, expels him from their adventuring party. At his lowest point, Ceres encounters Shizuko, who happens to be Zect’s mother. Rather than returning to his old group, he sees an opportunity to pursue his long-standing dream of building an adventuring party made up of older women.

Seven Seas released the manga’s fifth volume on August 11, 2026, during the period covered by BookWalker’s chart. The series mixes revenge, fantasy-party politics, and deliberately outrageous romantic comedy, making its premise clear from the title alone. Its ninth-place position shows that the unusual isekai setup found a sizable audience on the platform.

Masamune Shirow’s The Ghost in the Shell is the ranking’s classic cyberpunk entry. First serialized in 1989, the manga follows Major Motoko Kusanagi, a cyborg operative who investigates cybercrime in a future where human bodies and machines increasingly overlap. Its cases raise questions about artificial intelligence, memory, identity, and what makes a person human.

The landscape of anime and manga fandom has utterly changed in the post-pandemic years, so here's what you should check out next!

The franchise’s staying power is easy to understand, becuase its mix of tactical action and philosophical science fiction helped shape generations of cyberpunk storytelling. Kodansha also brought the work back into focus in 2026 with a Legacy Edition box set, featuring an uncensored presentation and new artwork from Shirow. The manga placed tenth on BookWalker’s summer chart.

The MCU Ends Its Best Trilogy Since James Gunn's Guardians Of The Galaxy

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Trilogies work best when there’s a single, unifying vision behind them — George Lucas’ Star Wars trilogy, Sergio Leone’s Dollars trilogy, Peter Jackson’s Lord of the Rings trilogy — but there are so many cooks in the kitchen at Marvel Studios that singular visions are rare in the MCU. Typically, a series of solo movies will switch hands between a bunch of different directors with a bunch of different styles, going off in whatever direction the larger cinematic universe dictates. Thor’s trilogy started off with the Shakespearean heft of Kenneth Branagh and ended with the ironic self-awareness of Taika Waititi. Captain America’s trilogy is a pulpy World War II actioner followed by a paranoid conspiracy thriller followed by Avengers 2.5.

It’s rare that the MCU is able to deliver a perfect trilogy with a clear beginning, middle, and end, and no weak links. The Thor trilogy is let down by The Dark World. The Ant-Man trilogy is let down by Quantumania. The Deadpool trilogy is spread across two separate franchises. Tom Holland’s Spider-Man trilogy never had a weak link, but Far From Home is pretty unremarkable.

As it stands, the best trilogy in the MCU is James Gunn’s Guardians of the Galaxy series. Gunn wrote and directed all three Guardians movies, so they’re all held together by the same creative voice. The second movie has some pacing issues, and some not-so-subtle symbolism in the father-son story, but that’s the worst thing you could say about it. The first film was a breath of fresh air, the second one maintained the emotional weight to complement the comic book spectacle, and the third one was the best of the bunch, with a much deeper and darker story to tell.

With the release of the VisionQuest finale, the MCU has now completed its best trilogy since Guardians of the Galaxy. The WandaVision trilogy of streaming shows has been much more consistent and satisfying than most of the MCU’s movie trilogies.

WandaVision was a massive success, both critically and commercially, when it premiered in 2021. Marvel put its best foot forward in the brave new post-Endgame world, and audiences responded with such enthusiasm that this little sitcom homage became a cornerstone of the Multiverse Saga. WandaVision remained a one-and-done limited series, but it was followed up by two sequel shows.

After WandaVision told the story of Wanda Maximoff processing her grief over Vision’s death (in a roundabout way), Agatha All Along wondered if her witchy arch-nemesis Agatha Harkness could be redeemed, and VisionQuest wondered if sentient A.I.s and fabricated memories can enjoy lives and identities of their own. All three shows have been spectacular in their own way, and they’ve each made for a worthwhile chapter in the overall story.

Jac Schaeffer humanized Wanda and Vision in WandaVision — she took this witch and this robot who have fought supervillains with the Avengers and live in a magical sitcom fantasyland, and really dug into their emotional conflicts — and that spirit carried through into the subsequent spinoffs. Agatha All Along is as much about the fraught relationships between the witches in the coven as it is a campy homage to horror classics, and VisionQuest is as much a meditation on artificial intelligence as it is a quirky buddy comedy about bickering androids.

All in all, the WandaVision trilogy has been one of the strongest stories that the MCU has ever told, and it’s not over yet. The trilogy has ended, but VisionQuest’s post-credits scene seems to be setting Wanda up for a major role in Avengers: Doomsday. That final stinger showed that, after Wanda made the ultimate sacrifice to destroy the Darkhold in Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness, Doctor Doom showed up to steal her powers. So, we could be seeing the Scarlet Witch under Doom’s control.

Congress' incoming wealthiest member vows to crack down on politicians getting rich in office

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Florida congressional hopeful Joe Strada explains how he built a half-billion-dollar business, why he entered politics and what matters more to him than money.

EXCLUSIVE: Rep.-elect Joe Strada, R-Fla., poised to become the wealthiest member of Congress, told Fox News Digital that he plans to crack down on politicians who enrich themselves while serving in public office when he takes office in January.

Strada, who went from working as a bowling alley mechanic to owning a nationwide HVAC company, is expected to become Congress' richest member, according to Forbes. The incoming Republican argued that public office should be about serving Americans rather than accumulating personal wealth.

"We need to make sure that we hold politicians accountable," Strada told Fox News Digital. "Nobody, and I mean nobody, should get rich in public office. We're there to serve the American people."

Democrats regained majorities in both the House and Senate as a blue wave swept the country. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

"If you want to get rich, stay in the private sector. I went into business to get rich, not Washington, D.C. I'm going to Washington, D.C., to serve the American people," he continued.

The congressman-elect said combating congressional insider trading and political profiteering would be among his priorities as he prepares to leave the private sector for Capitol Hill.

Strada pointed to his own rise from a blue-collar worker to a successful businessman as proof that the American Dream remains achievable, arguing that excessive government intervention has made it harder for others to follow a similar path.

"I started with nothing. I was a handyman at a bowling alley. If I can do it, other everyday Americans can. Government needs to get out of their way and help them succeed," Strada said. "It's so important to give people opportunity to succeed and achieve their version of the American dream, and now it's time to open that up."

"That's what I plan to do in Washington, D.C., give every American the opportunity they need to succeed, prosper, open a small business and start a career," he added.

"I'm living proof of it," Strada said, referring to his own success.

Rep.-elect Joe Strada, R-Fla., said he plans to crack down on politicians who enrich themselves while in public office. (Fox News Digital)

The incoming Republican also warned that the growing popularity of democratic socialism and proposals for expanding government programs could undermine American entrepreneurship, innovation and small-business growth.

"I think it's important to show people the opportunity. The talking points of ‘free this, free that’ sounds great, but we do not want to suppress the American people. We instead want to show them that their goals and ambitions are achievable," Strada said.

"It was achievable for me. I never went to college, the thought of college was not even in conversation with my parents. Going to college for me was unachievable," he added.

Strada said his early work experience taught him the importance of discipline and perseverance, values he believes should be emphasized to younger generations.

"I knew I had to work, and I worked hard," Strada said, adding that young Americans could benefit from a greater emphasis on work ethic. "I think if we can instill that in young people today, they'd be better off."

The congressman-elect also identified housing affordability as a major obstacle preventing Americans from achieving financial independence, pledging to pursue federal deregulation, lower construction material costs and workforce development initiatives to make homeownership more accessible.

"Housing cost is ridiculous. I have so many examples of ways we can still build a high-quality home for substantially less money, and more Americans could live out their dream of owning a home," Strada said.

The U.S. Capitol is shown in Washington, D.C., on Jan. 7, 2026. (Al Drago/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

As he prepares to take office in January, Strada said he hopes to secure a seat on the House Appropriations Committee, where he believes his business experience would allow him to "best serve the American people."

Why hasn't the 'Blaze Star' exploded yet? Astronomers weigh in on what to expect

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

Space news, cosmic updates and expert insights straight to your inbox.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"T CrB *will* erupt sometime soon. Everyone is very confident on this."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

For the past two years, stargazers have waited with bated breath for a "new" light to appear in the night sky, born of one of the most cataclysmic events known to occur in the cosmos — a nova explosion. But what is the "Blaze Star" T. Coronae Borealis, and why is it so difficult to predict when its overdue brightening will occur? We turned to the experts to find out.

T. Coronae Borealis — or T CrB for short — isn't a single star, but rather a binary system containing a dense white dwarf star and a bloated red giant. Over a period of around 80 years, the white dwarf's gravity strips material from the red giant until it hits a critical threshold. Next comes a dramatic nova explosion that causes the star to flare to 1,000 times its usual brightness before gradually settling down and repeating the cycle.

"T CrB *will* erupt sometime soon," Bradley Schaefer of the Louisiana State University Department of Physics and Astronomy told Space.com in an email. "Everyone is very confident on this. But we cannot make high-accuracy measures of the rate of gas falling onto the surface of the white dwarf, and we do not have a high accuracy measure of the trigger mass for the big hydrogen bomb." Schaefer continued.

Historical observations reveal that the last nova eruption reached Earth in 1946, which places us well inside the window for another dramatic brightening of T. CrBb. However, the unpredictability of the white dwarf's feeding makes it difficult to pin down exactly when the explosion will become visible from Earth.

"The exact date of the upcoming T CrB eruption depends primarily on how fast the gas coming from its companion star is accumulating on the surface of the white dwarf," explained Schaefer. "When some specific trigger mass has accumulated, then T CrB will erupt. For the last 80 years, we have been watching the gas pile up on the white dwarf surface, and we know that it must be near the trigger threshold."

Astronomers have tried to predict T CrB's brightening by comparing a recent fluctuation in the light of the distant star system to a similar event that occurred in the runup to the 1946 event.

"There have already been some dimming and brightening episodes that caused excitement but did not yet turn into the main event," Elizabeth Hays, lab chief at NASA's Astroparticle Physics Laboratory at the Goddard Spaceflight Center, told Space.com in an email. "It is likely that the nova will happen in the next few years but we still don't know exactly when. I can't give you a guaranteed prediction, but I will be a little surprised if it gets to 2030, or 84 years past the last eruption."

Hays notes that the intense light from the red giant dramatically outshines the dimmer white dwarf, making detailed observation challenging. There is also no way to precisely measure the temperature and pressure of the matter lurking near the surface of the smaller star. "Predicting nova explosions are a little like predicting the weather; conditions may be right but the timing of the storm can be uncertain."

There'll be no visual warning of the eruption before it reaches Earth, though according to Hays there could be an early flash of light in the X-ray spectrum.

"The world's amateur astronomers have been doing an awesome job of keeping T CrB under hair-trigger monitoring roughly every minute or two, so when T CrB has brightened by around one magnitude, then alerts will spread around the world within minutes," said Schaefer.

Once the call goes out, telescopes and observatories around the world will kick into gear, running pre-prepared observation programmes to capitalize on the rare and cataclysmic event. "When the nuclear runaway process starts, it will take off and peak very quickly, in less than a day."

The nova will see the star jump from its normal brightness of magnitude +10 — which is beyond the detection limit of the naked eye — to magnitude +1.7, making it appear as if a star brighter than the North Star Polaris has suddenly burst to life in the night sky.

"The brightness will fall below naked-eye visibility in dark places within a couple of days and become too faint for binoculars within about a week," said Hays. "Outside the visible band, for example, in radio, X-ray and gamma rays, light caused by the eruption will be visible for months or even years. "

T CrB can be found glowing close to the magnitude +4.28 star Epsilon Coronae Borealis in the constellation Corona Borealis during the early evening in November, with the constellations of Hercules and Bootes on either side.

It will dip below the horizon for viewers in the U.S. just a few hours after sunset, before re-emerging into the northeastern sky in the predawn hours.

Want to capture a photo of T. CrB when it erupts? Then be sure to read our guide to photographing the night sky, along with our picks of the best cameras, lenses and smart telescopes that are perfect for capturing the majesty of the starry realm.

Anthony Wood joined Space.com in April 2025 after contributing articles to outlets including IGN, New Atlas and Gizmodo. He has a passion for the night sky, science, Hideo Kojima, and human space exploration, and can’t wait for the day when astronauts once again set foot on the moon.

You are at, I don't know, some sort of family dining experience. There is a stage. On the stage, there is a magician. You are called up, and made to select a card from a deck of 52. "Don't show me, don't show me", exclaims the sorcerer. There is a dramatic pause, and then he reveals—from his own pocket—your card. Polite applause.

He keeps going: from his other pocket, your wallet. From his shirt, your wedding ring. Under his hat, your pet hamster, clutching in its teeth a love letter you wrote to your wife when you were first dating. The magician removes his mask—he is you, and you are your wife. Also, throughout this process, someone has given you a backpack and keeps adding exotic hammers to it.

This is what playing Queen's Domain feels like.

Developed, somehow, by a team of two developers, Queen's Domain is a game of constant discoveries. From the moment you wash ashore on the Isle of Neasied, the game just keeps unfurling. It is like some monstrous origami—you unfold one part only to find within it 10 more seemingly impossible sub-folds. Within those? You know it, baby: sub-sub-folds. We are through the looking glass here.

All of Queen's Domain's levels are riddled with crevices, crannies, nooks, and hideaways. Going through them is an experience in seeing some distant ledge, suspiciously flat bit of wall, or oddly aligned geometry and asking yourself "Could I…?" You can. You almost always can, and the game's levels reward your ingenuity and curiosity with new weapons, stat buffs, and strange encounters with stranger NPCs.

Sometimes you unlock whole new regions. Sometimes this is not good. Requiescat in pace to all those who fell down a well and found themselves stranded forever in a beautiful but deadly zone they were approximately 40 levels too underpowered for.

It is the best Dark Souls 1 since Dark Souls 1: unforgiving but almost never unfair, except when being unfair would be funny, and forever dazzling you with masterstrokes of interwoven level design. 16 hours in and hacking my way through a major dungeon, I pulled a lever only to open a door that spat me out right at the game's starting area. I suspect a cross-section of this game's goliath, entwined map would make for a killer bit of wall art.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

Also, you kill like a billion things. Neasied and its sub-environs are filled with freaky creatures—including a fun screeching loot goblin—and it's up to you to put an end to them, which you do via the mechanism of your heaving sack of wunderwaffen. Here is where I warn you, reader: you might be a bit put off by the way Queen's Domain's combat functions at first.

It starts out stiff, stubborn, and idiosyncratic. Even basic enemies take a while to kill and you don't yet have all the tools to deal with them, mobility included. Meanwhile, mechanics like damage being commensurate with your stamina and every single enemy having a rock-paper-scissors-esque relationship to the game's damage types (they're weak to one, neutral to another, and resistant to a third) gives you a whole lot to think about as you try to figure out how to position yourself and swap between weapons without getting murdered.

Stick with it. It's the Morrowind thing: you need that first half hour (and it really is barely more than that) of struggle to appreciate the way your power scales as you unlock new weapons, acquire more abilities, and—most important—your understanding of the game deepens.

And if that doesn't tempt you, how about this: you like Pokémon? Because it's Pokémon, except your Pokémon are spears and clubs and mauls. You level every weapon independently from your own character level, and when they get high enough, a lot of them literally evolve, like Squirtle, into something new and weird.

At one point in my playthrough, I got stuck in an area for like three hours (I missed something obvious), and didn't even mind; it gave me time to level all three of the game's starting weapons into their bizarre, magical final forms.

I tell you, nothing has smitten me in 2026 like this. It is undoubtedly my personal game of the year, and it might be the first time I've felt that magic I first felt 13 years ago, ensconced in a first-year university flat, playing Dark Souls: Prepare to Die Edition on my PC for the first time.

Non-planar 3D printing has mostly been experimental, but PancakeSlicer could finally make more people interested in trying it out and find more ways to improve it and use it practically.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Alpaca Engineering posted a YouTube video yesterday showcasing his self-hostable non-planar slicer. He explains that most non-planar 3D printing — printing across all three axes, rather than in 2D horizontal layers — demonstrations are usually for research purposes, for demonstration and experimentation, with no easy way for most 3D printer owners to try out non-planar 3D printing. His project, named PancakeSlicer, aims to fix this by making his non-planar slicing algorithm as easy to implement as existing slicing workflows, even improving upon it with 3D printer management features.

Alpaca Engineering explains that every few months, a non-planar 3D printing demonstration goes viral, but is rarely adopted practically by most 3D printer owners. Most non-planar experiments are for research, with half-abandoned GitHub projects. He further explains that non-planar isn’t widely adopted not because there aren’t any general-purpose non-planar slicers, but because it’s not accessible and easily implemented in a familiar workflow, with 3D printer owners spoiled by modern workflows and not needing to manually run G-code directly from an SD card.

Commercially available 3D printers are also not optimized for non-planar 3D printing. The challenge with printing at various angles is the clearance angle needed to bring a print head up and down without crashing into its own print. The slicer uses 3D models of various print heads from different 3D printer models to know how to slice the object to avoid collisions, thus having limited support; but Alpaca Engineering has listed a large number of supported 3D printers from Bambu Lab, Prusa, Creality, Voron, and many others. The varying shapes of the print heads also mean different printers will have different capabilities, with some managing less angle and curvature than others.

Alpaca Engineering also has great animations and practical demonstrations of the different slicing algorithms in the slicer, including how the algorithm slices the object for non-planar printing. We highly suggest you watch the video if you’re interested, as his animations explain non-planar slicing intuitively.

One of the best features of this slicer is its self-hosting features. Users can host the slicer from a desktop, NAS, or a home server and access it with a web UI, allowing multiple users to send print jobs from any device. It also has 3D printer management features, applying different profiles to different printer brands, unifying the experience without hopping from one slicer to another to operate multiple 3D printers — this is even available to Bambu Lab users, replacing Bambu Studio and Bambu Connect in LAN-only mode. The slicer can also manage two slice jobs at the same time in case other users happen to be slicing at the same time.

While PancakeSlicer isn’t the first to implement self-hosted 3D printer management features, it certainly levels up his initial goal of making non-planar slicing as easy to access as most modern workflows. We hope to see how more people can experiment with non-planar 3D printing, contribute their findings, and rapidly improve upon it as a genuinely practical slicing method.

Why your laptop fans spin up even when you're not doing anything

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It could point to many things, including aggressive power profiles, background software updates or even dust building up.

Laptops generate heat. You can't really avoid it. Even energy-efficient models are still producing some level of heat. To try and keep things cool, your laptop fans should kick into action when you're doing something that pushes it hard, like gaming. By design, most laptops will ship with adjustable fan speeds that keep temperatures cool without too much noise. You'd usually expect the fans to quiet down, although they won't necessarily stop completely.

That isn't always the case, though. You might hear your laptop fans spin up even when you've closed all your apps. Power settings can have an impact, but so can dust and debris clogging up your case. You'll need to work out whether the noise is normal, because if it isn't, you'll need to fix it.

Closing the windows on your desktop doesn't stop everything happening on your laptop. Apps run in the background, with running processes that continue performing tasks under the hood without you realizing. These all create demands on your processor, RAM and storage. More power means more calls for the fan to spin.

Dell, for instance, recommends reviewing your startup apps to keep the loads down on your CPU, which should help prevent your fans from spinning up unnecessarily. You can review these in Task Manager by pressing Ctrl + Shift + Esc, selecting Startup apps, and disabling anything non-essential.

Other essential maintenance tasks will cause demands while your PC isn't active, too. Windows Update will do it, as will search indexing, which catalogs your files so that you can easily search for them later. That initial indexing process can take a few hours, according to Microsoft. The same applies to MacBooks, and you can use Activity Monitor to see which background apps are using the most power.

Imagine a set of scales. You can tip them one way or another, or get them perfectly balanced. Your laptop power settings work the same way. On a Windows laptop, for instance, you can switch between modes that prioritize energy efficiency, performance or a balance between the two. The most aggressive option, for best performance, will prioritize speed over saving power.

This can allow for higher power consumption under load, which'll produce more heat and make the fans more likely to kick in. You can change these settings on Windows, for instance, via Settings > System > Power & battery > Power mode. These settings will change when you've plugged in your laptop compared to when it's running on battery, too.

Your laptop might come with an app that lets you set your own cooling settings, even if you've changed Windows' own settings. For example, some Dell laptops ship with an ultra performance mode that'll increase processing power and cooling fan speeds. Switching to quiet mode reverses it, although that will reduce performance and push the temperatures up when you're using your laptop.

You'll only know what settings work best, as far as cooling is concerned, by trying them out. Try each setting at a time, then push it through your usual use. You'll want performance mode if you're doing something like gaming, because you'll want maximum power and cooling. For lighter work like typing up documents, you might manage better by switching to balanced or energy efficiency modes.

The amount of work you're doing on your laptop is only really part of the explanation. Your surroundings can have an impact. Apple, for instance, suggests that higher ambient temperatures will force fans to spin up sooner and run faster. A warm room makes it harder for your laptop to stay cool, and it'll produce different fan behavior as a result than a laptop in a cooler space.

It isn't just the air, though. Where you place your laptop is important, because you need good ventilation for your fans to lower temperatures and, as a result, work properly. Working from bed isn't great for the air from your laptop fans to circulate, for instance. With restricted airflow, temperatures will rise and the fans will keep working harder to try and compensate.

A hard, flat desk is a better option for the vents on your fan, but even better is if you can raise it up a little to give more space for the air to flow. Side and rear openings matter, too. Manufacturers like Dell and Apple recommend keeping any ventilation openings unblocked for the best performance.

Then there's dust and dirt. Air flowing through your laptop isn't just bringing in the air; it's bringing in the dust that might be in the air. This builds up over time. It collects around the vents and inside the cooling fans, and around the fan blades themselves. It raises the baseline, meaning your fans have to work harder and harder, even when you're performing the same tasks. If you keep your laptop fans clean from dirt, it'll help the air flow freely so the fans don't have to work as hard.

A good can of compressed air will help. You might be able to take the bottom panel from your laptop off to give it a more thorough clean, but in most cases, you might not need to. Use the can liberally to push as much of the dust out of your fans as possible to clean your laptop fan.

Changing the settings on your laptop isn't a miracle fix. If your fans are still spinning hard, even when your laptop isn't doing much, then you'll need to investigate a little deeper.

Don't rule out a hardware issue. If the laptop maintenance has fallen behind, you might start to hear persistent grinding or rattling, which would point to a worn-out or damaged fan. Cleaning won't help if that's the case. You'll need to check whether your laptop is still under warranty, or if it isn't, switch the fan out yourself.

Check that there aren't any software issues, though. Get the BIOS updates installed and any manufacturer-issued apps, like Dell's Dell Power Manager, to give you full control over the power settings. HP recommends that you have the latest BIOS update installed, along with chipset and graphics drivers, as your GPU can impact fan speeds.

Apple users can reset their System Management Controller if they're seeing unexpected heavy fan usage on an Intel MacBook, for instance. For newer Apple silicon MacBooks, the advice is a little simpler: just close and reopen the lid, or give it a restart.

There are some manufacturer-issue tests you can run through. For example, some Dell laptops have fan tests that you can work through during preboot diagnostics, which you can access by restarting the laptop, pressing F12 when the Dell logo appears, and then selecting Diagnostics.

You don't need to worry every time your laptop fans make themselves heard, though. Background tasks can kick them into action, but unless the noise is distracting, or you're hearing grinding or rattling, then you can usually leave them to do their job, as long as your laptop isn't overheating. Changing the settings and cleaning the vents could help, but if it doesn't, give your warranty a check and see if you can get your laptop checked for damage.

“I would have preferred for that not to be the case, but it’s entertainment. I didn’t get to control that edit,” Evans tells Gutman in a preview of Sunday’s interview. He adds that his primary motivation for participating was to show audiences a more personal side of Holmes, who is currently serving a federal prison sentence for fraud.

Viewers can watch the interview live on Paramount+ with a Premium subscription, which costs $13.99 per month and includes access to a live feed of their local CBS station. The streaming service’s ad-supported Essential plan, priced at $8.99 per month, also offers CBS programming on demand, with new episodes generally available the day after broadcast.

Sunday’s broadcast, hosted by Jane Pauley, will also feature interviews with U2 marking the band’s 50th anniversary, John Travolta discussing his upcoming film “November 1963” and author Min Jin Lee on her latest novel, “American Hagwon.”

Suspected China spy linked to Nvidia's ex-boss left in 2025, Megaspeed says

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Nvidia scandal has sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley as well as the political establishment

Alice Huang, a suspected China spy who has been linked to Nvidia's former boss Jensen Huang, left her executive role at Megaspeed in August last year, the company says.

Jensen Huang resigned as chief executive of the AI chip-making giant Nvidia after it emerged he had allegedly had affairs with Alice Huang as well as with one of his employees.

Megaspeed is the biggest buyer of Nvidia chips in south-east Asia and the scandal at Nvidia has raised questions about the possibility that high-end AI chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

The Singapore-based company said on Sunday it had launched an internal investigation into its business.

It said it was aware of reports "containing assertions about our business, supply chain and global operations that are not supported by verifiable evidence".

It added that it "conducts legitimate and lawful business" and has "retained external legal counsel to conduct a thorough internal investigation".

On Friday, US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent blamed fellow Trump cabinet colleague Howard Lutnick's commerce department for failing to properly oversee the export of high-end chips.

Bessent told CNBC that several agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months... but Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process".

The so-called entity list restricts US trade with overseas firms and individuals.

Lutnick has been called to testify before the Senate Banking Committee next month over his department's role in allowing Nvidia to export its advanced chips.

Other congressional committees have asked both Lutnick and his department to retain all records relating to Nvidia and Jensen Huang.

Megaspeed is suspected of giving backdoor access to Nvidia chips to Chinese companies via its data centres in Malaysia, Indonesia and Thailand.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Nvidia has been banned from selling its advanced chips to China.

While Singapore does not prohibit the transfer of advanced AI chips to companies abroad, such moves can breach US export controls.

Singapore has said that while it does not enforce "unilateral export controls imposed by other countries" it expects all companies in its jurisdiction to respect such regulations.

Megaspeed said it "designs and maintains its global operational and supply-chain control frameworks to comply with all applicable laws and regulatory regimes". It said this includes "US export control requirements and local jurisdiction rules in every market where we operate".

China has denied that Alice Huang is a spy and warned the US not to use the scandal as a "pretext for imposing a technological blockade" on AI technology.
